9 Data Selection

Lecture slides for this chapter

Purpose

Architecture stack focus for this chapter.

How can a carefully selected subset retain most of the value of a much larger dataset?

Training pays to acquire, label, score, move, and process examples even though their learning value differs. Large datasets contain redundant, noisy, or target-misaligned samples, while scarce regions may lack the cases a model needs, such as the rare conditions under which a deployed system is most likely to fail. Training on every example treats a near-duplicate and a rare failure case as equally worth paying for, and at scale much of the compute budget can go to relearning what the model already knows. That heterogeneity turns the choice of examples into a systems optimization rather than a data-cleaning chore. The objective is therefore not the smallest dataset but the lowest end-to-end cost of reaching target quality without losing rare cases, underrepresented groups, or deployment-relevant coverage. Every strategy adds its own overhead: examples must be scored, generated, shuffled, stored, or validated, and those costs can erase the savings they promise. That judgment must be made end to end, because gains in labeling or training can be erased by selection latency, storage access, or distributed coordination. A method is worth applying only when the learning it preserves or creates exceeds the work required to apply it. This distinction matters across repeated experiments, where useful reductions compound, and across adaptation, where the value of an example changes with the model and target distribution. In D·A·M terms, it is data-algorithm co-design: shape the training signal so each unit of machine work yields more useful learning.

Learning Objectives
  • Explain data selection as data-algorithm co-design that controls sample value, training work, and labeling cost
  • Calculate the information-compute ratio and apply the compute-optimal tokens-per-parameter check to diagnose a data-starved run
  • Apply the selection inequality to test whether selection overhead beats full-dataset training cost
  • Compare deduplication, coreset selection, and quality pruning for reducing redundant pretraining data
  • Design curriculum, active learning, synthetic-data, or label-free strategies for changing data value during training
  • Evaluate whether a selection pipeline keeps the accelerator fed under storage locality, data echoing, and multi-accelerator consistency constraints
  • Select data-selection investments using return on investment, break-even, and amortization analysis
Explore the concepts in this chapter

The volume’s concept map, opened on this chapter. Select a concept to read how it connects to ideas in other chapters. Open the full map ↗

9.1 The Data Wall

Every example a training run processes costs the same forward pass, backward pass, and optimizer update, whether it teaches the model something new or repeats what the model already knows. A duplicated image, a mislabeled record, and a rare boundary case cost the same arithmetic while contributing very different learning signals. Model Training made each of those steps as cheap as one machine allows and accounted for a whole run as roughly \(6\Parameters\DatasetSize\) floating-point operations for \(\Parameters\) parameters and \(\DatasetSize\) tokens. Once each step is cheap, the next saving lies upstream, in choosing which examples deserve a step at all. This waste becomes acute at the data wall, the point at which the compute available for training grows faster than the supply of novel, high-quality examples to spend it on.1

1 Data wall: Unlike compute (which scales with capital expenditure) or algorithms (which improve through research), the stock of high-quality human-generated text grows slowly; Epoch AI’s updated projections estimated that, under the paper’s modeled consumption assumptions, models could use datasets roughly equal in size to the stock of public human-generated text between 2026 and 2032 (Villalobos et al. 2024). The point for systems design is not a fixed calendar deadline; it is that data can become a supply constraint rather than merely an economic one. The wall does not change how many operations \((\Operations)\) a run performs. It lowers the learning each of those operations yields, because once quality data runs short, additional compute spent on repeated or weaker data yields diminishing accuracy regardless of hardware throughput.

Villalobos, Pablo, Anson Ho, Jaime Sevilla, Tamay Besiroglu, Lennart Heim, and Marius Hobbhahn. 2024. “Position: Will We Run Out of Data? Limits of LLM Scaling Based on Human-Generated Data.” Proceedings of the 41st International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML), Proceedings of machine learning research, vol. 235: 49523–44.

Compute outruns high-quality data supply; the widening gap is the data wall.

2 Scaling laws: Jared Kaplan and colleagues at Johns Hopkins and OpenAI empirically demonstrated in 2020 that language model loss follows power-law relationships with model size, dataset size, and compute budget over the regimes they studied (Kaplan et al. 2020). Their fitted data exponent was approximately \(\Glyph{\alpha} = 0.095\) in \(\LossFunction \propto \DataScalingLossFactor\). The value is not universal, but the diminishing-return shape makes it possible to reason about when selection becomes more cost-effective than collection.

Kaplan, Jared, Sam McCandlish, Tom Henighan, Tom B. Brown, Benjamin Chess, Rewon Child, Scott Gray, Alec Radford, Jeffrey Wu, and Dario Amodei. 2020. “Scaling Laws for Neural Language Models.” ArXiv Preprint abs/2001.08361.

Scaling laws2 (Kaplan et al. 2020; Hoffmann et al. 2022) quantify how model performance improves with dataset size, and teams have responded by collecting ever larger corpora, yet accelerator capacity expands faster than human-generated text and expert annotations. Much of the accessible public web has already been incorporated into large training sets, and specialist labeling grows slowly. Data Engineering measured the growth of training corpora, and Training Data Approaching the Stock of Public Text sets the largest of them against the estimated stock of public text, between \(10^{14}\) and \(10^{15}\) tokens, which they reach late this decade at the fitted rate. That is the wall at frontier scale, on fleets far larger than one machine, and it shifts engineering effort from collecting everything available to extracting more learning signal from each stored and processed sample.

↩︎ GPT-2, the bandwidth-bound lighthouse for decoder-only language models, is established in Autoregressive decoding.

Radford, Alec, Jeffrey Wu, Rewon Child, David Luan, Dario Amodei, and Ilya Sutskever. 2019. Language Models Are Unsupervised Multitask Learners. OpenAI.

The full GPT-2 XL run that Model Training costed on one H100 shows the same wall on a single machine. WebText, GPT-2’s training corpus (Radford et al. 2019), holds 40 GB of text, about 10 billion tokens at roughly 4 bytes per token. The compute-optimal balance fitted by the Chinchilla study, about 20 training tokens per parameter (developed in section 9.2.3), asks for 30 billion unique tokens for GPT-2’s 1.5 billion parameters, 3× what WebText holds. The original run spent about 1.5 × 10²¹ FLOPs, which at six FLOPs per parameter per token processed about 167 billion tokens, so the model saw its corpus roughly 17 times. One machine with 8 H100 accelerators running at 50 percent model FLOPs utilization (MFU), the fraction of peak arithmetic a training run actually delivers (definition 8.3), completes that budget in about 4.4 days and 590 kWh of accelerator energy. Less than a week of one machine’s compute therefore passes over the whole corpus many times. An engineering team in that position can repeat epochs over the same text, where gradient updates on familiar tokens yield diminishing loss reductions, or it can lower quality thresholds to admit uncurated web text, where noisy and misaligned tokens degrade downstream evaluation. Instead of accepting either penalty, the team can invest in data selection (refining quality filters, constructing difficulty curricula, and synthesizing missing edge cases) to raise the learning return per FLOP.

When compute is abundant and quality data is scarce, the optimization priority inverts. Selection, not faster kernels, then extracts more learning per dollar, and for teams whose accelerator budget exceeds their curated corpus, the bottleneck shifts from accelerator access to the quality, legality, and diversity of the training data.

The bitter lesson applies here in data terms. A filter written from human intuition about which examples matter is the hand-built expertise the lesson warns against, whereas selection that uses the model’s own signals, such as loss, gradient norms, or embedding similarity, scales with compute. Either way, improved rankings must be established against downstream quality and coverage.

↩︎ The bitter lesson, that general methods using more computation outperform encoded human expertise, is established in The Bitter Lesson.

Data engineering makes data correct; data selection asks which correct examples are worth training on. When selection removes processing at matched quality, it shrinks the Data factor (\(\text{Samples/Task}\)) and therefore the total operations \((\Operations)\) term in the iron law (principle 3). Selection therefore needs sample value stated relative to compute cost, and the information-compute ratio of section 9.2.1 measures that value as learning signal gained per unit of training compute.

Definition 9.1: Data selection

Data selection is the process of retaining, prioritizing, or generating training examples to minimize the end-to-end cost of reaching target quality while preserving required coverage.

  1. Significance: Each excluded example removes a forward pass, a backward pass, and an optimizer update, so selection reduces \(\text{Samples/Task}\) and therefore total operations \((\Operations)\) directly.
  2. Distinction: A dataset can pass every data engineering check and still contain many examples that teach the model nothing new; selection judges informativeness and diversity, not correctness.
  3. Common pitfall: Minimizing dataset size instead of end-to-end cost. Scoring, labeling, and lost coverage can erase the savings of a smaller set.

Holding epoch count, batch size, and per-sample work fixed, a 50 percent reduction in dataset size directly halves the required forward passes, backward passes, and gradient updates. For the GPT-2 run above, it would halve both the 4.4 days on one eight-H100 machine and the 590 kWh of accelerator energy, provided the training schedule does not compensate by adding optimization steps. When data selection is combined with model compression and hardware acceleration, those reductions compound across all three axes of the iron law.

Systems Perspective 9.1: Data selection and the iron law
In the multiplicative D·A·M form of the iron law of ML systems (equation 1.2, where \(\Time = (\text{Samples/Task}) \times (\text{FLOPs/Sample}) \times (\text{Seconds/FLOP})\)), data selection reduces the first factor (\(\text{Samples/Task}\)). Model-level optimizations reduce the second (\(\text{FLOPs/Sample}\)). Machine-level optimizations reduce the third (\(\text{Seconds/FLOP}\), by increasing \(\PeakRate \cdot \EfficiencyHw\) and cutting memory and latency stalls). Selection removes work before those downstream optimizations execute it.

Because the three D·A·M factors multiply, their speedups compound. A 2× reduction in dataset size with 2× fewer operations per sample and 2× higher effective machine throughput yields an 8× reduction in run time because each axis divides the work or time the next axis sees. The machine factor unpacks into the three physical terms of equation 1.3 (\(\Time = \frac{\DataVolume}{\Bandwidth} + \frac{\Operations}{\PeakRate \cdot \EfficiencyHw} + \Latency\)), which state the condition for realizing that full product. Halving the number of training steps cuts all three terms in half. The algorithmic and hardware speedups, however, achieve their full multiplier only when the dominant physical bottleneck falls in step, whether that bottleneck is compute, data movement \((\DataVolume/\Bandwidth)\), or fixed overhead \((\Latency)\).

These compute savings propagate throughout the hardware hierarchy. By decreasing total data volume (\(\DataVolume\)), deduplication and coreset selection alleviate storage bandwidth demands, network fabric ingress, and host-to-accelerator PCIe transfers that otherwise leave the accelerator’s arithmetic units waiting on input. Upstream, active learning curbs annotation budgets by directing human labeling to samples chosen for uncertainty, diversity, or other task-relevant query criteria.

Yet selection is not free. Every selection strategy incurs its own runtime overhead to score, filter, shuffle, or coordinate samples. An online selection algorithm that executes additional forward passes to rank sample difficulty can consume more accelerator cycles than it saves if its selection overhead exceeds the subsequent reduction in training time. A pipeline claiming a training-time saving must make filtering plus subset training faster than full-data training at the same target quality, the selection inequality of section 9.2.6. Labeling savings and reusable representations require the broader cost accounting of section 9.8.

A learning-theory view of this problem counts the samples needed to reach a test error and treats each sample as free. The systems view counts the FLOPs, bytes, labels, seconds, and joules needed to reach the same quality, including the cost of choosing the samples. In the D·A·M taxonomy, data selection works on the data axis, deciding whether an example warrants processing at all before the algorithm fixes the operations each example costs and the machine fixes how fast those operations run. Removing an example therefore lightens both adjacent boundaries, the storage and input traffic between data and machine and the learning return between data and algorithm.

↩︎ The D·A·M taxonomy and its three diagnostic axes are established in Defining ML Systems.

The engineering toolkit for intelligent data selection follows a deliberate optimization ordering, first asking whether a sample is worth processing and only then how to process the remaining workload efficiently. Static pruning acts first by fixing the retained subset before target-model training begins, even when its scoring step uses a smaller proxy model. Because a sample’s usefulness changes as the model learns, dynamic selection then adapts which examples the training loop sees and which receive human labels during training through curriculum learning and active learning. Where real data still falls short after pruning and reordering, synthetic generation creates missing high-value samples on demand through augmentation, simulation, or examples labeled by a larger trained model (a teacher). Learning from unlabeled data complements these three levers by redefining what counts as a supervision signal. Rather than selecting among labeled examples, semi-supervised and self-supervised methods derive learning targets from the structure of unannotated data. Which levers to use, and in what order, depends on the workload (figure 1).

Two lighthouses carry the chapter, each in its own role. GPT-2 XL, bandwidth-bound when it decodes one token at a time but compute-bound when it trains on long batches (Model Training), carries the wall itself, the compute-optimal diagnosis of whether a run is data-starved, and token-level deduplication. ResNet-50 on ImageNet, the book’s compute-bound lighthouse, carries coresets, labels, augmentation, the input pipeline that must feed it, and the decision of which lever to fund. Its training crosses into an input-bound regime whenever the host cannot decode images as fast as the accelerator consumes them (Pipeline stages and accelerator bubbles), and that crossing returns when selection runs on the machine. The measures of value in the next section use both, and both run on the book’s reference machine, the H100, so every saving can be stated in accelerator seconds and joules. The recommendation lighthouse (DLRM), MobileNetV2, and keyword spotting return only in the closing comparison of section 9.8, where each turns out to be bound by a different resource.

Every lever in the chapter removes work at one stage of the machine and adds work of its own, so each must pass a test before it is accepted (table 1). Each test in the right-hand column depends on measuring what an example is worth, which is the subject of the next section.

Self-Check: Question

  1. In an ML infrastructure scaling scenario where available GPU compute grows by approximately \(10\times\) every 3 years while high-quality web data grows by only \(2\times\) every 5 years, what primary systems regime emerges, and what is the appropriate systems response?

    1. A compute-rich, data-constrained data wall regime where intelligent data selection and curation must maximize the learning signal extracted per token
    2. A memory bandwidth-bound regime where model parallel sharding must replace data parallelism across all training clusters
    3. An I/O ingestion bottleneck where disk read bandwidth must be quadrupled to keep GPUs saturated
    4. A compute-starved regime where synthetic data generation should be eliminated to avoid wasting accelerator cycles

  2. True or False: Doubling the amount of raw, deduplicated web data will always double the total information learned by the model.

  3. Order the following stages as they are presented in this chapter’s conceptual map of the data-selection pipeline: (1) Dynamic Selection, (2) Static Pruning, (3) Synthetic Data Generation.

See Answers →

9.2 Measuring Data Value

A ResNet-50 trained on an aggressively filtered ImageNet subset can hold its aggregate accuracy while failing on rare classes that the filter discarded as low-density noise. A GPT-2 run on a filtered corpus can cost less per epoch while needing more epochs to reach the same loss. A measurement framework is therefore needed to show what a reduction actually saves. Every data selection technique claims to improve efficiency, but only measurement separates genuine training savings from work that has merely moved to another pipeline stage, or from degradation on safety-critical tail distributions. The measures build from the information-compute ratio through a diagnostic of whether a run is data-limited, metrics that tie sample reduction to accuracy, and checks that a reduced set still covers the cases deployment needs, to the selection inequality that every training-time claim must satisfy.

9.2.1 The information-compute ratio

Both failure modes bound a Pareto frontier (principle 5) between accuracy and cost, the set of operating points where retaining every example spends FLOPs on redundant signal and pruning further sacrifices rare-slice recall. Locating a run on that frontier requires measuring how much learning signal each sample yields per unit of hardware work. Data engineering already measured task-relevant signal against one cost, the data gravity of moving bytes, in the data selection gain of equation 4.3. Selection puts training compute in the denominator instead, because a sample that is cheap to move still costs a full forward and backward pass. That ratio is the information-compute ratio (ICR).

↪ Locating a model’s operating point on the accuracy-efficiency frontier, and moving along it with compression, is developed in The accuracy-efficiency frontier.

Let \(\FiniteChange\DataInformationContent\) be the learning signal a sample or batch adds and \(\FiniteChange\Operations\) the training operations spent processing it. In practice \(\FiniteChange\DataInformationContent\) is measured as the gain in a fixed evaluation metric, such as percentage points of validation accuracy, so ICR carries units of metric gain per FLOP. The ratio relates the two: \[\text{ICR} = \frac{\FiniteChange\DataInformationContent}{\FiniteChange\Operations}\]

A higher ICR indicates that each FLOP of training yields more measured learning signal over the stated interval. Validation gain and training FLOPs can be observed for a specified experiment with a fixed evaluation protocol; the causal contribution of an individual example generally cannot. Uncertainty and gradient-based scores instead rank candidate examples before their downstream benefit is known. Those rankings require validation against measured quality, compute cost, and deployment-critical slice coverage. The core metrics in section 9.2.4 supply complementary evidence for that evaluation rather than interchangeable measurements of per-example information, and they also track how ICR decays as the dataset grows.

9.2.2 Diminishing returns and the ICR frontier

As a training run adds more examples from the same source, a growing share of those examples repeat patterns the model has already seen, and the information-compute ratio falls. The ICR frontier is the dataset size beyond which the marginal ICR, the learning returned by the next FLOP spent on more data, drops below what that FLOP could return elsewhere: a larger model, a different data source, or an earlier stop.

To illustrate diminishing returns, let \(\DataInformationContent(\DatasetSize)\) be the information content of a dataset of size \(\DatasetSize\) and assume \(\DataInformationContent(\DatasetSize) \propto \log \DatasetSize\). This is a simplified analytical model rather than a universal law. If compute cost scales linearly with the per-sample operation count \(\OperationsPerSample\), then total operations are \(\Operations = \OperationsPerSample \cdot \DatasetSize\), and the resulting marginal ICR follows equation 1: \[\text{ICR}(\DatasetSize) = \frac{\DerivativeOperator\DataInformationContent/d\DatasetSize}{d\Operations/d\DatasetSize} \approx \frac{1/\DatasetSize}{\OperationsPerSample} = \frac{1}{\OperationsPerSample\cdot \DatasetSize} \tag{1}\]

The derivative ratio in equation 1 describes the return on an infinitesimal increase in dataset size, whereas for two controlled finite allocations the corresponding incremental ratio divides the additional evaluation gain by the additional training FLOPs under the same evaluation metric and protocol. Under this illustrative model, marginal ICR falls as \(1/(\OperationsPerSample \cdot \DatasetSize)\), so doubling the dataset halves the learning each additional FLOP yields. Inverted, the compute spent per unit of learning grows linearly with \(\DatasetSize\). ICR never reaches zero in this model and the curve has no knee of its own, so the frontier is located by comparing marginal ICR with the threshold the engineer sets, not read off the curve. That saturation is distinct from the data wall (a limit on how much new, high-quality data exists), but the two compound. When new data is scarce and marginal data is weak, selection becomes the main lever. Beyond the frontier, additional data still adds compute but returns less learning than the threshold demands. In this regime additional data inflates the \(\Operations\) term of the iron law without a commensurate gain in ICR. Measured in dollars rather than FLOPs, the same excess lowers the return on compute (RoC) of equation 1.6, the accuracy gained per added dollar of infrastructure.

↩︎ Return on compute, the economic lens that measures accuracy gains against infrastructure spending, is established in Return on compute: Weighing accuracy against cost.

Data selection turns the total operations \((\Operations)\) term in the iron law from a fixed constant into a variable. A 2\(\times\) improvement in measured ICR halves the FLOPs required to reach the same measured gain. It produces the same ideal time reduction as a 2\(\times\) throughput increase only when the workload is compute-bound and the saved work lies on the critical path. ICR focuses specifically on compute; the cost model of section 9.8.1 extends the same reasoning to acquisition, labeling, and storage costs.

The ratio becomes concrete on a coreset, a deliberately selected subset intended to preserve the full dataset’s learning signal. Computing ICR for an ImageNet coreset used to train the ResNet-50 lighthouse shows how large the efficiency gap could become once dataset size, per-sample model cost, and selection strategy meet concrete FLOP budgets. The comparison averages gain over one epoch of each dataset rather than measuring the incremental return on the next data slice, and it uses assumed accuracy gains to illustrate the arithmetic rather than a reported result for the scoring method (EL2N) that section 9.3.4 develops.

Napkin Math 9.1: Computing ICR: Coresets
Problem: Training the ResNet-50 lighthouse model on ImageNet for one epoch costs 3.2 × 10¹⁶ FLOPs. Under the scenario’s assumed accuracy gains, how would retaining 50 percent of the data change the information-compute ratio? The calculation holds the per-sample work fixed and does not include selection overhead.

Setup:

  • Dataset: ImageNet (1.28M)
  • Model: ResNet-50 lighthouse (~8.2 GFLOP per forward pass, roughly 24.6 GFLOP for a forward plus backward training step, depending on implementation)
  • One epoch: 1.28M \(\times\) 24.6 GFLOP = 3.2 × 10¹⁶ FLOPs
  • Assumed accuracy improvement per epoch (early training): 5 percentage points

Random selection (baseline):

  • Process all 1.28M samples uniformly
  • Accuracy gain: 5 percentage points
  • \(\text{ICR}_{\text{random}}\) = 5 percentage points / (3.2 × 10¹⁶ FLOPs) = \(1.6 \times 10^{-16}\) per FLOP

Difficulty-scored coreset (EL2N, a training-dynamics score defined in section 9.3.4; 50 percent of data):

  • Process 640.6K selected samples
  • Assumed accuracy gain: 4.5 percentage points (90 percent of the full-data gain)
  • Compute: 640.6K \(\times\) 24.6 GFLOP = 1.6 × 10¹⁶ FLOPs
  • \(\text{ICR}_{\text{coreset}}\) = 4.5 percentage points / (1.6 × 10¹⁶ FLOPs) = \(2.9 \times 10^{-16}\) per FLOP

Systems insight: Under these assumptions, the selected subset achieves 1.8× higher ICR with a 0.5 percentage points smaller accuracy gain. The comparison is learning per FLOP over one epoch of each dataset. It does not show that the coreset reaches the full-data run’s final accuracy, which must be checked separately at equal quality, and a real decision also requires measured gains, selection overhead, and coverage checks.

Checkpoint 9.1: Data selection efficiency

The goal is to improve ICR subject to target quality and required coverage, then verify that the resulting pipeline reduces the cost it is intended to address.

Metric checks

Pipeline checks

A reduction in sample count remains a claim until it is shown to translate into end-to-end efficiency without silently compromising generalization. Notebook 9.1 holds per-sample work fixed and assumes its accuracy gains. Testing the claim on a real workload first requires knowing whether the run is limited by data at all, and then measured metrics that weigh arithmetic savings, data movement overhead, and task quality across the complete pipeline.

9.2.3 The compute-optimal frontier

Pruning a dataset when the model is already starved for unique tokens hurts accuracy rather than saving time, whereas collecting more data for an under-trained model wastes money on collection. Before evaluating any selection technique, a team must therefore test whether the training run is limited by data or by compute. Neural scaling-law experiments (Kaplan et al. 2020; Hoffmann et al. 2022) fit power-law relationships over particular model families, datasets, and compute ranges. Controlled sweeps can use those fitted relationships to compare model and data allocations, though no single ratio diagnoses every training run.

3 Chinchilla: Chinchilla is a 70-billion-parameter language model trained on 1.4 trillion tokens. In the experiments reported by Hoffmann et al. (2022), it outperformed the larger GPT-3 on most evaluated tasks under a similar training-compute budget. The fitted compute-optimal allocation scaled parameters and tokens at roughly equal rates within the study’s experimental regime; it is not a universal prescription for every architecture, dataset, or training recipe.

Hoffmann, Jordan, Sebastian Borgeaud, Arthur Mensch, Elena Buchatskaya, Trevor Cai, Eliza Rutherford, Diego de Las Casas, et al. 2022. “Training Compute-Optimal Large Language Models.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 35: 30016–30. https://doi.org/10.52202/068431-2176.

The Chinchilla study3 (Hoffmann et al. 2022) fit a compute-optimal balance between model size and training data within its experimental regime. Allocations on either side of that fitted balance used the study’s compute budget less effectively. The optimal balance defines a compute-optimal frontier: the best achievable performance at each compute budget when data and model size are properly balanced. Plotted over normalized training compute, the frontier separates the optimal operating envelope from the compute-starved and data-starved allocations in the suboptimal region below it (figure 2).

The frontier answers one allocation question, whether the next unit of compute at a given budget should go to more parameters or to more training tokens. A run is data-starved when it trains on too few unique, useful tokens for its parameter count, so its tokens-per-parameter ratio sits well under the fitted balance, and the same compute would yield more accuracy with more or better data. Against a frontier fitted from controlled sweeps, an operating point’s location motivates further experiments. The directional vectors charted in figure 2 illustrate two candidate interventions: additional training compute and improved data selection at fixed compute. Controlled changes to model size, data, and training allocation determine which intervention helps; a point below the frontier does not identify a unique remedy. Points near a fitted frontier remain specific to the model family, dataset, objective, and compute range used to estimate it.

Interpreting the Chinchilla result

Within the fitted Chinchilla regime, compute-optimal model parameters and training tokens grew at roughly equal rates.4 Training a dense transformer costs approximately \(6\Parameters\DatasetSize\) FLOPs for \(\Parameters\) parameters and \(\DatasetSize\) tokens, the forward-plus-backward accounting derived for the GPT-2 lighthouse in Running cases: GPT-2 XL and ResNet-50. Under an equal scaling regime where parameters and tokens grow proportionally (\(\Parameters \propto \DatasetSize\)), total compute scales quadratically (\(\Operations \propto \DatasetSize^2\)), directly implying that optimal token count scales as the square root of the compute budget (\(\ComputeOptimalTokenCount \propto \sqrt{\Operations}\)). Doubling compute therefore corresponds to about \(\sqrt{2} - 1\), or 41.4 percent, more tokens under those assumptions. The commonly quoted 20 tokens per parameter is a useful reference point from that study, not a universal optimum.

4 Tokens per parameter: The ratio \(\DatasetSize/\Parameters\) compares training tokens with model parameters; GPT-3 used approximately 1.7 tokens per parameter (Brown et al. 2020), while Llama 2 70B used approximately 28.6 (Touvron et al. 2023). These descriptive ratios do not establish whether either run is compute-optimal under a different architecture, corpus, objective, or hardware budget. Controlled model-size and token-count sweeps are required for that diagnosis.

Brown, Tom B., Benjamin Mann, Nick Ryder, Melanie Subbiah, Jared Kaplan, Prafulla Dhariwal, Arvind Neelakantan, et al. 2020. “Language Models Are Few-Shot Learners.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems 33: 1877–901. https://doi.org/10.48550/arxiv.2005.14165.
Touvron, Hugo, Louis Martin, Kevin Stone, Peter Albert, Amjad Almahairi, Yasmine Babaei, Nikolay Bashlykov, et al. 2023. “Llama 2: Open Foundation and Fine-Tuned Chat Models.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2307.09288.

GPT-2 sits well short of that reference. WebText supplies about 6.7 unique tokens per parameter, a third of the fitted balance, and the original run made up the difference by repeating those tokens about 17 times (section 9.1). Repetition keeps the arithmetic budget but not the learning per FLOP, which is the regime where selection, rather than more compute, moves the run toward the frontier.

Applying the diagnostic

If a training run underperforms expectations, extending one run can reveal whether additional optimization steps still help, but a plateau does not identify data starvation by itself. Learning-rate schedules, optimization limits, model capacity, and data quality can all produce a plateau. A reliable diagnosis compares controlled runs that vary model size, token count, and compute allocation while holding the evaluation protocol fixed. When controlled runs do point to the data, the remedy that keeps compute fixed is better selection, which changes the shape of the learning curve itself. Seeing that change requires switching axes, from the frontier’s training compute to dataset size, where the core metrics measure what a reduction preserves.

9.2.4 Core metrics

Reporting accuracy at a single subset size hides whether a selection method learned faster early on, plateaued lower, or never reached the full dataset’s target quality. Three metrics track those distinct behaviors across the learning curve and complement the measured information-compute ratio (section 9.2.1). Performance-per-data (PPD) measures average accuracy gain per sample, which becomes gain per FLOP once per-sample cost is fixed. The area under the learning curve (AULC) summarizes performance across a common dataset-size range. The data compression ratio (DCR) measures retained sample reduction at a fixed target accuracy. None of the three establishes the causal value of an individual example.

Performance-per-data

The most direct metric, performance-per-data (PPD), measures accuracy gain per sample: \[ \text{PPD}(\LearningCurveSampleCount) = \frac{\text{Accuracy}(\LearningCurveSampleCount) - \text{Accuracy}(0)}{\LearningCurveSampleCount} \] where \(\LearningCurveSampleCount\) is the number of training samples. A higher PPD indicates greater average improvement per sample over the stated interval. Its shape must be measured because diminishing returns and their onset are workload-dependent.

Area under the learning curve

Rather than comparing at a single point, the area under the learning curve (AULC) integrates performance across all dataset sizes: \[ \text{AULC} = \int_0^\DatasetSize\text{Accuracy}(\LearningCurveSampleCount) \, d\LearningCurveSampleCount\] where \(\LearningCurveSampleCount\) is the dataset size and \(\DatasetSize\) is the total dataset size.

For the same metric and integration range, a higher AULC means stronger integrated performance across dataset sizes, though one strategy need not outperform the other at every size. Comparisons must use the same upper limit \(\DatasetSize\) or normalize the integral. Measuring AULC requires training at several dataset sizes, so the metric itself spends compute, and when it guides a selection decision that cost belongs to the selection overhead.

In the illustrative curves of figure 3, efficient selection bends into an accuracy plateau near 25 percent of the data while uniform random sampling climbs gradually across the entire budget. Over the same range, the efficient curve therefore encloses the larger area. The horizontal separation between the curves is the reduction in required samples, and at an identical 20 percent data budget the vertical gap is the accuracy that selection adds without any additional compute.

Data compression ratio

For coreset methods, the data compression ratio (DCR) measures how much data reduction is achieved at a target accuracy: \[ \text{DCR} = \frac{\FullDatasetSize}{\SubsetDatasetSize} \text{ at } \text{Accuracy}_{\text{target}} \]

A DCR of 5\(\times\) means the coreset achieves target accuracy with 20 percent of the data. Applied to the coreset notebook, the three metrics behave differently. Over one epoch, the full ImageNet set gains 5 percentage points while the half-size coreset gains 4.5 percentage points, so the coreset’s PPD is higher by the same 1.8× as its ICR, because per-sample work is fixed. DCR cannot be computed from that notebook at all, because the two runs never reach a common target accuracy. Exposing that missing equal-quality check is exactly the job DCR is designed for. Even when a reduced dataset matches the full dataset’s aggregate accuracy, those learning-curve metrics cannot show whether the retained subset still covers every slice deployment needs.

9.2.5 Validating a reduced dataset

When the half-size ImageNet coreset drops 640,000 images, it can hold top-1 test accuracy while quietly stripping out a rare class, shifting the training distribution away from production traffic, or concentrating mislabeled examples in the retained subset. Validating a curated dataset therefore requires checking coverage, distribution alignment, and label quality alongside aggregate accuracy. Coverage metrics check whether coreset selection kept enough examples from each class and demographic group. Even when class counts hold, the retained subset can skew toward atypical examples, so distribution alignment metrics (such as KL divergence and the population stability index, the drift statistics of Data drift detection and response) test whether the curated training set has drifted from the deployment distribution. Where selection also guides annotation, label quality metrics (inter-annotator agreement and confident learning, which flags examples whose given label disagrees with a model’s confident out-of-sample prediction) verify that active learning did not introduce systematic labeling errors. A 50 percent dataset reduction is worth keeping only when all three checks pass alongside target accuracy, calibration, and robustness.

↪ Coverage, label-quality, and distribution-alignment checks are developed into a full data-benchmarking protocol in Data benchmarking.

The validation target can itself be unreliable. Benchmark accuracy can overstate generalization when test sets fail to capture operational distribution shifts, demanding the same scrutiny for evaluation benchmarks as for curated training sets.

Example 9.1: The benchmark replication gap
Scenario: Classifiers evaluated on newly collected test sets experienced 3 to 15 percentage-point accuracy drops on CIFAR-10 and 11 to 14 points on ImageNet (Recht et al. 2019).

Diagnosis: The replicated collection produced slightly harder images than the original test sets. The study found that benchmark adaptivity did not explain the gap, even though repeated use of a fixed test set remains a general evaluation risk.

Systems lesson: Evaluation-set construction affects measured generalization. Data-selection pipelines should validate curated subsets on independently collected and deployment-relevant test sets rather than trusting one long-reused benchmark.

Recht, Benjamin, Rebecca Roelofs, Ludwig Schmidt, and Vaishaal Shankar. 2019. “Do ImageNet Classifiers Generalize to ImageNet?” Proceedings of the 36th International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML), 5389–400.

An efficiency gain measured against a single benchmark can evaporate under distribution shift, so a curated subset must demonstrate consistent quality across multiple held-out distributions before its reported reduction ratio is trusted. Even when quality holds across those distributions, the time spent selecting the subset must still be weighed against the training time it saves.

9.2.6 The selection inequality

The efficiency measures so far count learning against training compute alone, yet a selection method also spends time choosing, and a claim that it shortens training must count that time. To reduce overall training time at a fixed quality target, selection plus subset training must take less time than training on the full corpus. Let \(\SelectionTime\) be total wall-clock selection overhead, including candidate scoring and any prerequisite scorer preparation charged to the training run. Let \(\TrainingTime(\DatasetSize)\) be wall-clock training time for a dataset of size \(\DatasetSize\), and let \(\SubsetDatasetSize\) and \(\FullDatasetSize\) be the retained and full sample counts. Equation 2 formalizes this break-even condition as the selection inequality: \[ \SelectionTime+ \TrainingTime(\SubsetDatasetSize) < \TrainingTime(\FullDatasetSize) \tag{2}\]

Equivalently, isolating the allowable selection overhead yields \(\SelectionTime< \TrainingTime(\FullDatasetSize) - \TrainingTime(\SubsetDatasetSize)\). The upper bound on selection latency depends strictly on how much training execution the subset eliminates; no arbitrary overhead fraction applies across workloads. When an engineering pipeline can amortize scorer preparation or checkpoint extraction across multiple downstream training runs (section 9.8.3), \(\SelectionTime\) reflects only the marginal scoring pass over the candidate pool. Conversely, if an auxiliary proxy must be trained from scratch for a single isolated run, that preparatory compute adds directly to \(\SelectionTime\). When \(\SelectionScoreFunction(\ModelInput)\) repeatedly scores the candidate pool with the full target model, those extra scoring passes and their cross-worker coordination can take longer than the training steps they remove, making the run slower overall.

Example 9.2: Selection inequality in practice
Scenario: A computer vision team selects a 128,117-image coreset (10 percent) from the 1.28 million ImageNet training images to shorten ResNet-50 training on one H100 sustaining 25 percent of its peak, the training chapter’s compute-bound rate. Pretrained checkpoints for both the target architecture and a lightweight proxy are already available from earlier exploration, so this comparison accounts only for a marginal scoring pass over the candidate pool.

Diagnosis: Against a 211-minute full-data run of 100 epochs, marginal full-model scoring (Option A: target ResNet-50) adds 42 seconds of selection overhead and still saves 188.8 minutes (89.7 percent). Marginal proxy-model scoring (Option B: ResNet-18) drops selection overhead to 18 seconds and saves 189.1 minutes (89.9 percent), only 24 seconds more.

Systems lesson: Both options satisfy the selection inequality by a wide margin, because a single scoring pass costs at most one forward pass over the dataset (one-third of a full-data epoch for the target ResNet-50) and less with the proxy. Training on the 10 percent coreset also shrinks every one of the 100 epochs. Scorer choice also becomes decisive when selection rescores dynamically every epoch or when the scoring architecture is significantly larger than the target, multiplying selection latency while subset training savings remain fixed.

End-to-end wall-clock time divides into selection overhead and subset training, and the split decides the outcome across three regimes (figure 4). Lightweight proxy scoring limits selection overhead to 5 percent while training on the 40 percent subset, which yields 55 percent net savings. Heavyweight scoring expends 60 percent overhead on selection, which wipes out all subset-training efficiency gains.

This overhead compounds when selection runs dynamically across epochs rather than once offline. If per-epoch rescoring approaches the training work saved by the pruned subset, the net systems advantage vanishes. Measured this way, a selection strategy is judged against its stated objective, counting the overhead of scoring, filtering, and staging samples alongside the training, labeling, or reuse savings it claims at target quality. Teams that optimize selection algorithms against isolated benchmarks rather than end-to-end pipeline constraints find that intuitive heuristics backfire: scoring overhead consumes accelerator cycles, optimization trajectories destabilize, or generalization silently degrades. These measures apply to the three levers in the order in which they act, and static pruning comes first because it fixes the subset before target-model training, though its scoring process may use a trained proxy.

Self-Check: Question

  1. Under the illustrative analytical model where dataset information content scales as \(\mathcal{I}(D) \propto \log D\) and training compute scales linearly as \(O = O_{\text{sample}} \cdot D\), how does the marginal Information-Compute Ratio \(\mathrm{ICR}(D)\) scale with dataset size \(D\)?

    1. It remains constant at \(\mathcal{O}(1)\) because additional compute scales proportionally with dataset size
    2. It decays as \(\mathcal{O}\!\left(\frac{1}{O_{\text{sample}}D}\right)\), so under this illustrative model additional data eventually adds compute faster than marginal information
    3. It grows logarithmically as \(\mathcal{O}(\log D / O_{\text{sample}})\) due to power-law parameter scaling
    4. It decays exponentially as \(\mathcal{O}(\exp(-D))\) once the training corpus exceeds accelerator memory capacity

  2. Explain why a dataset where \(100\%\) of the sample labels are verifiably correct can still exhibit a very low Information-Compute Ratio (ICR).

  3. An ML systems diagnostic plot maps normalized training compute on the horizontal axis against model performance on the vertical axis. A 10M-parameter run sits well below a fitted frontier under a fixed evaluation protocol and fixed total budget. A controlled sweep then shows that reallocating the same budget toward a larger model improves performance. What is the most defensible diagnosis of the original operating point?

    1. Data-starved regime
    2. I/O bandwidth-saturated regime
    3. The model/data allocation was suboptimal under the tested budget
    4. Over-echoing regime

  4. A team wants one metric for how much smaller a selected dataset is at a target accuracy, and another for how much learning is gained per sample over a measured interval. Which pair of chapter metrics matches that need?

    1. Data Curation Rate and Accuracy Reduction Ratio
    2. Data Compression Ratio and Performance-Per-Data
    3. Dynamic Checkpoint Rate and Active Retention Rate
    4. Data Convergence Ratio and Amortized Risk Ratio

  5. Why is Area Under the Learning Curve (AULC) a more informative metric than single-point final validation accuracy when comparing data selection methods across dataset sizes?

  6. A training team reduces a dataset from \(1{,}000{,}000\) to \(100{,}000\) images. Full-data training takes 10 hours, subset training takes 1 hour, and scoring the full pool with the production model takes 12 hours. Which conclusion and next step best fit the selection inequality?

    1. Yes, the inequality is satisfied because a \(90\%\) dataset reduction guarantees positive ROI
    2. Yes, because the 9 hours saved in training outweigh the scoring cost; the team only needs more GPUs
    3. No, because \(12+1=13\) hours exceeds the 10-hour baseline; a plausible fix is proxy scoring or cached embeddings, but the team must verify that scoring drops below 9 hours while target quality holds
    4. No, because coreset training always increases memory-bandwidth consumption; the team should switch from NVMe SSDs to HDDs

9.3 Static Pruning

Web-scale scraping routinely gathers boilerplate text, duplicate images, and malformed records, and each one costs GPT-2 six FLOPs per parameter for every token, or ResNet-50 a full forward and backward pass, before a single useful gradient is calculated. Static pruning removes these low-value records upstream of the training loop. By dropping redundant and corrupt records before target-model training begins, static pruning shrinks the operation count \((\Operations)\) without changing model architectures or execution kernels. Once empirical learning curves establish that a corpus carries removable redundancy and noise, a static pipeline strips out exact and near duplicates first, filters corrupted or mislabeled records next, and ranks the remaining clean pool with coreset algorithms.

9.3.1 The case for smaller datasets

On CIFAR-10, Paul et al. (2021) report that EL2N pruning (which scores each example by its prediction error early in training) removed half of the training data without reducing accuracy, and on ImageNet-1K Sorscher et al. (2022) report that a self-supervised prototype metric discarded 20 percent of the data without sacrificing performance. These empirical pruning studies confirm that dataset volume alone does not determine model accuracy, because large corpora carry substantial redundancy where many examples contribute almost no gradient signal while still costing a full training step. The pattern extends to language modeling, where web-scraped corpora like The Pile5 and C46 contain enough duplicate and templated content to make deduplication a systems issue. In the datasets studied by Lee et al. (2022), deduplicated training reduced memorization while preserving or improving perplexity. Still, these reported gains are benchmark-specific: pruning effectiveness depends on how redundant the dataset is, which selection algorithm is used, and the capacity of the target model, so teams must validate aggressive pruning on their own task before committing to it.

Sorscher, Ben, Robert Geirhos, Shashank Shekhar, Surya Ganguli, and Ari S. Morcos. 2022. “Beyond Neural Scaling Laws: Beating Power Law Scaling via Data Pruning.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 35: 19523–36. https://doi.org/10.52202/068431-1419.

5 The Pile: An approximately 886 GB English text corpus (reported as 825 gibibytes by the source) aggregating twenty-two sub-datasets, including PubMed, ArXiv, GitHub, Project Gutenberg, Common Crawl, Stack Exchange, Wikipedia, and USPTO patents (Gao et al. 2020). Its multi-source design makes it a useful example of data diversity: each source family has a different duplication, quality, and domain-coverage profile, so selection pipelines must preserve coverage while removing redundant text.

Gao, Leo, Stella Biderman, Sid Black, Laurence Golding, Travis Hoppe, Charles Foster, Jason Phang, et al. 2020. “The Pile: An 800GB Dataset of Diverse Text for Language Modeling.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2101.00027.

6 C4 (Colossal Clean Crawled Corpus): C4 applies filtering to Common Crawl data, including language detection, deduplication of repeated three-sentence spans, and removal of pages that fail heuristic quality checks, producing approximately 750 GB of cleaned English text (Raffel et al. 2020). Filtering has its own compute cost and can remove useful material, so its ROI and quality effects must be measured rather than inferred from corpus size alone.

Raffel, C., N. Shazeer, A. Roberts, K. Lee, S. Narang, M. Matena, Y. Zhou, W. Li, and P. J. Liu. 2020. “Exploring the Limits of Transfer Learning with a Unified Text-to-Text Transformer.” Journal of Machine Learning Research 21 (140): 1–67.

Individual data points need not provide equal value for training. This heterogeneity follows in part from how classifiers learn decision boundaries. Many samples fall far from a boundary: a picture of a dog in good lighting may become easy once the model has learned its dominant features, while ambiguous and underrepresented cases can remain informative. Label quality also affects data requirements. Conflicting labels can push updates in opposing directions, so reaching a target error may require more examples or more passes over them. The size of that penalty depends on the task and training procedure. A data quality multiplier therefore comes from comparing learning curves at the same held-out error, rather than assigning a universal convergence rate to all noisy data.

Napkin Math 9.2: The data quality multiplier
Problem: Two hypothetical pilot learning curves reach the same held-out error using different amounts of clean and noisy data. With epoch count and per-sample work held fixed, what training-work multiplier do these sample counts imply?

Setup: Consider a representative evaluation where a fixed model architecture and training recipe reach target error \(\TargetError\) = 0.01 (1 percent) under clean versus noisy training partitions:

  • Clean data: \(\CleanLabelSampleCount\) = 10,000
  • Noisy data: \(\NoisyLabelSampleCount\) = 16,000

Result: Dividing the noisy-data count by the clean-data count gives 1.6×. Under the fixed training recipe, the noisy dataset therefore requires 1.6× as much training work to reach the same error.

Systems insight: The avoided training work sets a budget for cleaning. Cleaning pays for itself only when correction or review costs less than the work it removes, with required coverage preserved. If the two learning curves require different epoch counts, compare total examples processed to the target instead of unique sample counts. The decision depends on measured learning curves and pipeline costs, not on the presence of noise alone.

9.3.2 Data deduplication

The cheapest samples to remove are the ones that repeat what the dataset already contains. Repeated content costs a full forward and backward pass on every presentation, even when that repetition adds little useful learning signal. Exact hashing and shingle-based near-duplicate detection can identify candidates for removal without training a scoring model. Deduplication therefore comes first among the static levers, but near-duplicate thresholds must be validated so the pipeline does not remove useful distributional signal.

The simplest form of deduplication uses hash-based methods for exact matches. By computing a cryptographic hash (MD5 or SHA-256) for each sample and removing those with identical hashes, practitioners can eliminate the byte-for-byte duplicates that accumulate in large web-scraped corpora. This process is computationally cheap, scaling linearly with dataset size, and can be parallelized trivially.

↩︎ Deduplication as a deterministic data-cleaning step that keeps training and serving pipelines consistent is established in Training-serving consistency.

7 MinHash: Invented by Broder (1997) to detect duplicate web pages for AltaVista, the algorithm creates compact signatures using random hash functions such that similar documents produce similar signatures with high probability. Each signature compresses a document to a fixed-size sketch, enabling pairwise similarity estimation in \(\mathcal{O}(\MinHashSketchSize)\) time for sketch size \(\MinHashSketchSize\).

Broder, A. Z. 1997. “On the Resemblance and Containment of Documents.” Proceedings. Compression and Complexity of SEQUENCES 1997 (Cat. No.97TB100171), 21–29. https://doi.org/10.1109/sequen.1997.666900.

8 Locality-sensitive hashing (LSH): LSH hashes MinHash signatures into buckets so that similar signatures are more likely to collide. Candidate generation avoids an exhaustive all-pairs comparison, while runtime and memory depend on signature width, banding, bucket sizes, and the number of candidates that require verification.

9 Jaccard similarity: Defined as \(|\FirstJaccardSet\cap \SecondJaccardSet| / |\FirstJaccardSet\cup \SecondJaccardSet|\) for two sets \(\FirstJaccardSet\) and \(\SecondJaccardSet\), ranging from 0 (disjoint) to 1 (identical). For deduplication, the metric compares sets of shingles across documents of different lengths. The threshold must be calibrated for the shingle definition, corpus, and downstream quality because no universal cutoff separates duplicates from legitimately related documents.

Near-duplicate detection addresses content that differs at the byte level while retaining substantial token- or character-shingle overlap. For text, MinHash7 with Locality-Sensitive Hashing8 (LSH) approximates Jaccard similarity9 efficiently. In practice, the pipeline converts each document into a set of \(\ShingleLength\)-shingles, which are contiguous spans of \(\ShingleLength\) characters or words, turning sequence comparison into set overlap. It then compresses each shingle set into a fixed-length MinHash signature, where the probability that two documents agree at a signature position equals their Jaccard similarity. Comparing two short signatures is fast, yet comparing every pair of signatures across \(\DatasetSize\) documents would still require \(\mathcal{O}(\DatasetSize^2)\) work. To avoid that all-pairs scan, LSH partitions each signature into \(\LshBandCount\) bands of \(\LshRowsPerBand\) rows and hashes each band into a bucket. Documents that match across all \(\LshRowsPerBand\) rows of any single band collide in the same bucket, restricting pairwise similarity verification to likely candidates across a corpus of \(\DatasetSize\) web documents numbering in the billions. This pipeline detects lightly edited content with high lexical overlap, though it does not establish semantic paraphrase equivalence.

For images, perceptual hashing produces signatures robust to minor transformations like resizing and compression, identifying visually similar candidates stored in different formats. Embedding-based similarity can retrieve semantic near-neighbor candidates by computing dense representations (CLIP10 (Radford et al. 2021) for images, sentence transformers for text), though proximity does not itself prove duplication and the approach incurs higher computational overhead. Across both modalities, deduplication can reduce repeated-content memorization and avoid spending FLOPs on exact or near duplicates. It can also alter the corpus’s intentional empirical weighting, so thresholds and downstream quality must be validated rather than assuming that every duplicate is harmful.

10 CLIP (Contrastive Language-Image Pretraining): Pretrained on 400 million image-text pairs, CLIP maps semantically related images and text into a shared embedding space (Radford et al. 2021). Embeddings can retrieve candidates for review, but semantic proximity is not proof that two samples are duplicates. Generating embeddings also costs substantially more than computing perceptual hashes, with the ratio depending on model and hardware.

Radford, Alec, Jong Wook Kim, Chris Hallacy, Aditya Ramesh, Gabriel Goh, Sandhini Agarwal, Girish Sastry, et al. 2021. “Learning Transferable Visual Models from Natural Language Supervision.” International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML), 8748–63.

On GPT-2’s run, deduplication costs little and returns hours of accelerator time. Lee et al. (2022) found that near-duplicate removal affected between 3.04 percent (C4) and 13.63 percent (RealNews) of the corpora they studied. If WebText carried a comparable share, removing it would cut the same fraction of the 167 billion tokens GPT-2 processed. The saving would be about 3 hours to 14 hours of the 4.4 days that one eight-H100 machine needs, or 18 kWh to 80 kWh of accelerator energy. Finding the duplicates reads the 40 GB corpus once, about 6 seconds at the 7 GB/s of the machine’s local NVMe storage. The search also holds a 128-hash MinHash signature for each of WebText’s roughly 8 million documents, about 4.1 GB of host memory. Hashing runs on host CPUs rather than on the accelerators, so the cost of deduplication lands on a different stage of the machine from the work it removes. The open question for a corpus this size is which near-duplicates are safe to remove, not whether the machine can afford to find them. Once those duplicates are gone, the next target is records that are unique but broken.

9.3.3 Data pruning by quality

A unique training example can still waste compute or actively damage the model if its content is garbled or its label contradicts the true class. Quality-based pruning removes samples that carry no usable signal or push gradients in the wrong direction. In supervised datasets, label error detection is often the highest-return form of quality pruning. Tools like Cleanlab identify samples where the assigned label may be incorrect, based on model confidence patterns across training. A sample that the model consistently predicts as class A while its label says class B may be a hard boundary case, a distribution shift, a model blind spot, or an annotation mistake. Because those causes call for different remedies, the score should trigger review rather than automatic deletion, so confirmed errors can be corrected or removed without discarding hard examples.

Where label error detection checks whether the target contradicts the input, outlier removal checks whether the input itself sits far from any cluster center in feature space. Such points can indicate sensor noise, annotation errors, or file corruption. In heavy-tailed domains, however, they can also be the rare edge cases that define deployment risk, so outlier thresholds must stay conservative and the affected slices should be reviewed before removal.

Unlabeled pretraining corpora have no class labels to audit, so low-information filtering instead applies domain-specific heuristics to drop samples that carry too little signal to justify a training step. For text corpora, pipelines remove high-perplexity garbled strings (perplexity measures how surprising a text is to a language model, so high values can flag incoherent text as well as valid specialist or rare-language writing) alongside low-perplexity boilerplate or repetitive templates. For image datasets, they filter blurry, corrupted, or near-uniform frames. Because hand-written heuristics can miss subtler quality differences across billions of documents, industrial pretraining pipelines pair them with classifier-based filtering. This approach trains a lightweight linear classifier (such as fastText) with curated reference text (e.g., Wikipedia, books, and scientific papers) as positive examples and uncurated web dumps as negative examples. The classifier assigns each scraped document a continuous quality score, allowing the pipeline to drop millions of low-quality pages at gigabyte-per-second ingestion speeds before ranking the clean examples that remain.

9.3.4 Coreset selection algorithms

Once duplicates and defective records are gone, the remaining samples are unique and valid but still unequal in value, which is the heterogeneity described in section 9.3.1. Ranking them requires systematic selection criteria. Coreset selection11 turns the static pruning decision into a coverage problem: keep the smallest subset that preserves the statistical properties of the entire dataset. The systems decision is where to spend the selection budget, whether on cheap coverage metrics that preserve distributional structure or on costlier training-dynamics scores that better target the decision boundary. That decision also needs a guardrail before any score is applied: classes, demographic groups, time windows, and rare failure modes that matter at deployment require minimum representation, because the highest-average-ICR subset can still remove the examples that define production risk.

11 Coreset (core set): In computational geometry, a small subset can approximate a specified geometric objective within a controlled error factor (Agarwal et al. 2005). Machine learning adapts this idea as a selection principle, but a guarantee for an embedding and distance objective does not by itself guarantee neural-network accuracy. The retained subset must still be validated on the target task.

Agarwal, Pankaj K., Sariel Har-Peled, and Kasturi R. Varadarajan. 2005. “Geometric Approximation via Coresets.” In Combinatorial and Computational Geometry, edited by Jacob E. Goodman, János Pach, and Emo Welzl, vol. 52. MSRI Publications. Cambridge University Press. https://doi.org/10.1017/9781009701259.002.

12 k-center algorithm: Its greedy strategy iteratively picks the point farthest from the existing centers under a chosen embedding and metric. Sener and Savarese use this core-set framing for convolutional-neural-network active learning (Sener and Savarese 2018). The resulting coverage bound applies to that geometric objective, not directly to downstream accuracy or rare-class preservation.

Sener, Ozan, and Silvio Savarese. 2018. “Active Learning for Convolutional Neural Networks: A Core-Set Approach.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).
Welling, Max. 2009. “Herding Dynamical Weights to Learn.” Proceedings of the 26th Annual International Conference on Machine Learning, 1121–28. https://doi.org/10.1145/1553374.1553517.

Geometry-based methods select samples that cover a chosen representation without requiring target-model training. The \(\CenterCount\)-Center algorithm,12 a facility-location-style objective, selects samples that reduce the maximum distance from any point to its nearest selected center under the chosen embedding and metric. Herding takes a different approach: Welling’s original procedure iteratively constructs representative pseudo-samples whose feature statistics approximate observed moments (Welling 2009), and related moment-matching objectives can guide subset selection from a fixed pool. These methods are computationally attractive because they operate on feature representations, but their scores are not inherently label-aware.

Training-dynamics methods use a proxy model to identify examples that may be important. GraNd (Gradient Normed) and EL2N (error L2-norm)13 score samples by gradient magnitude or prediction error early in training (Paul et al. 2021). The exact alternative, influence functions (Koh and Liang 2017), measures how removing a sample changes validation loss. Yet it requires the inverse Hessian \(\LossHessian^{-1}\) of the loss across all \(\Parameters\) parameters, with \(\mathcal{O}(\Parameters^2)\) memory and \(\mathcal{O}(\Parameters^3)\) arithmetic, which rules it out for models with millions or billions of parameters.

13 EL2N (error L2-norm) and GraNd (gradient normed): These methods score samples based on their error or gradient norm early in training, identifying samples the model finds most difficult. The practicality of this approach relies on transferability, where scores from a small proxy model can guide data selection for a much larger target model. For instance, a proxy trained for five epochs can generate scores to curate a dataset for a full 90-epoch production training run (Paul et al. 2021).

Paul, Mansheej, Surya Ganguli, and Gintare Karolina Dziugaite. 2021. “Deep Learning on a Data Diet: Finding Important Examples Early in Training.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 34: 20596–607.
Koh, Pang Wei, and Percy Liang. 2017. “Understanding Black-Box Predictions via Influence Functions.” International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML), Proceedings of machine learning research, vol. 70: 1885–94.

14 Forgetting events: This method identifies valuable examples by tracking when the model “forgets” them, transitioning from a correct to an incorrect classification during training. The central trade-off is the high cost of this analysis, which requires a full training run. However, the resulting importance scores can transfer from small proxy models to larger target models (for example, ResNet-18 to ResNet-50), enabling inexpensive proxy-based selection when ranking quality holds for the target task (Toneva et al. 2019).

Toneva, Mariya, Alessandro Sordoni, Remi Tachet des Combes, Adam Trischler, Yoshua Bengio, and Geoffrey J Gordon. 2019. “An Empirical Study of Example Forgetting During Deep Neural Network Learning.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).

Systems methods circumvent this barrier with lightweight first-order approximations. GraNd scores each sample by its expected gradient norm (\(\|\nabla_\ModelParameters\LossFunction\|_2\)), which avoids the Hessian but still requires per-sample backpropagation rather than a single batch-averaged backward pass. EL2N removes that backward pass as well. Under cross-entropy loss with a softmax output layer, the gradient of the loss with respect to the pre-softmax logits is the output residual \(\PredictedOutput - \LabelVector\), the difference between the predicted probability vector \(\PredictedOutput\) and the one-hot label \(\LabelVector\). Because upstream weight gradients scale with that logit error via the chain rule, early in training the parameter gradient norm correlates strongly with the output error norm \(\|\PredictedOutput - \LabelVector\|_2\). Practitioners can therefore score each example from a single forward pass without computing backward-pass gradients. Error and uncertainty still differ: a confident prediction of the wrong class produces a large EL2N error despite low predictive uncertainty. High scores identify examples the proxy finds difficult, though they can also flag mislabeled or outlying records and therefore need coverage and quality checks. The same work reports useful score transfer across several architectures, which allows a small proxy model to score candidates for a larger target model. A third training-dynamics signal, forgetting events,14 tracks how often a sample is correctly classified and later misclassified during training (Toneva et al. 2019).

Each algorithm in table 2 balances upfront scoring computation against downstream training reduction. Because geometry-based and training-dynamics methods optimize different proxies, their relative ranking depends on dataset scale, feature representations, target model capacity, and scoring budgets; a score is computationally justified only when downstream training savings exceed evaluation overhead.

Geometry-based methods prioritize coverage in a chosen feature representation, while training-dynamics methods use signals such as error or forgetting. Uncertainty sampling, which active learning uses as a query rule (section 9.4.2), concentrates the budget near the current decision boundary; EL2N instead ranks prediction error, which can also be high for confidently wrong examples away from the boundary. Figure 5 contrasts that boundary-focused allocation with uniform random sampling of the same five-point budget. It illustrates one family of selection rules; a geometry-based method such as \(\CenterCount\)-center would instead spread its picks to cover the feature space.

EL2N with a small proxy model offers a practical balance between scoring cost and selection quality when no pretrained scorer is on hand. The workflow trains a lightweight proxy for a few epochs, computes EL2N scores, and selects a subset subject to coverage and quality checks. Returning to the 10 percent ImageNet coreset from example 9.2 shows what changes when the team must train that proxy before ranking; whether the resulting subset preserves target accuracy is what validation must then establish.

Example 9.3: Coreset selection in practice
Scenario: Without the pretrained checkpoints assumed in example 9.2, the vision team must train a proxy from scratch to select its 128,117-image coreset (10 percent) from the 1.28 million ImageNet training images for ResNet-50.

Diagnosis: Naive random subsampling can miss rare classes and boundary examples. Training a lightweight proxy model for 5 epochs calculates EL2N error scores, prioritizing high-error examples while checking rare-class coverage.

Systems lesson: Proxy-based coreset selection replaces some full-data passes with targeted scoring compute. It accelerates full-model training only when the proxy ranking transfers and coverage checks preserve rare classes and edge cases.

Listing 1 turns proxy prediction error into reusable selected indices. The compute_el2n_scores function spends a small amount of probe compute to measure which samples still confuse a briefly trained model, and select_coreset retains the highest-scoring examples. This top-\(\Glyph{k}\) sketch needs quality and coverage checks before its subset replaces the full training pool. The scoring pass requires an unshuffled dataloader so each score position remains aligned with its dataset index.

Because the unconstrained select_coreset slice in listing 1 takes the global top-\(\CoresetBudget\) scores regardless of class, it can under-sample a rare slice whose examples score lower than the majority class; enforcing a coverage floor changes which indices the ranking retains. Consider disjoint classes A and B, a total coreset budget \(\CoresetBudget\) as in table 2, and task-validated minimum counts \(\FirstClassCoverageFloor\) and \(\SecondClassCoverageFloor\) for the respective classes. First reserve the highest-scoring \(\FirstClassCoverageFloor\) eligible examples from A and the highest-scoring \(\SecondClassCoverageFloor\) from B. Fill the remaining \(\CoresetBudget-\FirstClassCoverageFloor-\SecondClassCoverageFloor\) slots from the global ranking, skipping indices already reserved. The allocation is infeasible if \(\CoresetBudget<\FirstClassCoverageFloor+\SecondClassCoverageFloor\), if either class lacks enough eligible examples to meet its floor, or if the eligible pool is smaller than \(\CoresetBudget\); the pipeline must revise the budget or acquire missing coverage before training.

Suppose the unconstrained top-\(\CoresetBudget\) ranking contains only A examples and the budget can meet both floors. Reserving B’s floor replaces the lowest-scoring \(\SecondClassCoverageFloor\) members of that all-A subset with the highest-scoring eligible B examples, while the remaining slots still follow the score order. Coverage thus has an explicit opportunity cost in the selection score. Overlapping demographic or operational slices require joint constraint handling because one example may satisfy several floors; summing those floors as if the slices were disjoint can reject a feasible budget. Even a feasible allocation establishes only retained counts. Independent evaluation must still establish target quality and performance on the required slices.

Together, these three static pruning techniques (deduplication, quality filtering, and coreset selection) shrink \(\Operations\) before training begins, subject to the same fixed-schedule caveat as before: a schedule that adds compensating epochs or steps gives back part of the saving. Static pruning answers a question about what to keep, but it treats the answer as fixed, training every epoch on the same retained subset. A sample’s usefulness, however, can change as the model learns, because examples that challenge an undertrained model may become easy after sufficient gradient updates. Dynamic selection techniques address this limitation by adapting the training data at each stage based on what the model has already mastered.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A team wants to score a dataset cheaply before training a large production model. They plan to train a small proxy for a few epochs and rank examples by prediction error, knowing that confidently wrong predictions can still score highly. Which method best matches that plan and its trade-off?

    1. \(k\)-Center clustering on raw pixel inputs, because it guarantees zero-cost label-aware boundary identification
    2. Forgetting Events scoring on the full production model, because it requires no proxy architecture and computes in \(\mathcal{O}(1)\) time
    3. EL2N (Error L2-Norm) scoring with a briefly trained proxy model, accepting that high error can reflect hard examples or noise
    4. Herding on Gaussian-distributed features, because it completely avoids computing feature representations

  2. Why is Locality-Sensitive Hashing (LSH) with MinHash preferred over exhaustive pairwise Jaccard similarity comparison for large-scale text deduplication?

    1. MinHash LSH guarantees \(100\%\) precision in detecting semantic paraphrases across different natural languages
    2. MinHash eliminates the need to tokenize or shingle input documents before hashing
    3. Exhaustive pairwise Jaccard comparison requires training a deep neural network, whereas LSH is purely rule-based
    4. Exhaustive pairwise comparison requires \(\mathcal{O}(D^2)\) document comparisons, whereas MinHash LSH hashes compact signatures into sublinear candidate collision buckets

  3. True or False: Removing near-duplicate documents with MinHash LSH always improves model accuracy because duplicate data has zero statistical value in all training regimes.

  4. The training-dynamics coreset metric that measures the Euclidean norm of the difference between predicted class probabilities and the one-hot target vector early in training is known as ____.
