9.4 Dynamic Selection

Early in a ResNet-50 run over ImageNet, clean, centered images of common classes provide stable gradients that establish general feature representations, but as parameters converge their gradient norms decay toward zero. Executing forward and backward passes on these mastered samples continues to consume accelerator execution cycles and high-bandwidth memory (HBM) transfers while producing negligible parameter updates, driving their effective ICR toward zero. Dynamic selection mitigates this waste by adapting batch composition to the model’s current state. This shifts selection from an offline preprocessing step into the active execution loop, modifying the operation count (\(\Operations\)) at runtime. The same adaptive logic extends to labels, where active learning decides which examples receive a human label as the model’s uncertainty shifts.

9.4.1 Curriculum learning

Random shuffling feeds hard boundary cases, noisy labels, and outliers to an untrained model before its early features have stabilized, while still spending full epochs on simple examples long after the model has mastered them. Curriculum learning15 (Bengio et al. 2009; Soviany et al. 2022) orders the training stream instead of shuffling it uniformly, starting with simpler examples and gradually introducing harder ones as parameters converge. Ordering the stream changes which gradients dominate at each stage of training: easy examples provide stable early signals, while examples scored as hard can contain useful boundary information, label noise, or outliers. An easy-to-hard schedule can smooth training on some workloads, but its benefit depends on the difficulty score, pacing rule, model, and data; any claimed epoch reduction must therefore be treated as illustrative rather than guaranteed.

15 Curriculum learning: From Latin currere (“to run”), originally meaning “the course to be run,” a metaphor that maps directly to the technique, with training data as a course run in deliberate order and the easy stretches first. Curriculum learning can act as a continuation method for nonconvex optimization, with easier examples shaping the early optimization trajectory before harder examples are introduced. From a systems perspective, the ICR of a sample can vary during training, which motivates changing the mix of examples over time.

Bengio, Yoshua, Jérôme Louradour, Ronan Collobert, and Jason Weston. 2009. “Curriculum Learning.” Proceedings of the 26th Annual International Conference on Machine Learning, 41–48. https://doi.org/10.1145/1553374.1553380.
Soviany, Petru, Radu Tudor Ionescu, Paolo Rota, and Nicu Sebe. 2022. “Curriculum Learning: A Survey.” International Journal of Computer Vision 130 (6): 1526–65. https://doi.org/10.1007/s11263-022-01611-x.

Implementing a curriculum requires two components: a difficulty scorer that ranks samples, and a pacing function that controls how quickly hard samples are introduced. A common choice is linear pacing: \[ \text{samples}_{\EpochIndex} = \texttt{sort\_by\_difficulty}[:\DatasetSize\cdot \min(1, \EpochIndex/\WarmupEpochCount)] \] where \(\EpochIndex\) is the current epoch, \(\DatasetSize\) is the total dataset size, and \(\WarmupEpochCount\) is the number of warmup epochs before the full dataset becomes available. Early epochs train on the easiest fraction \(\EpochIndex/\WarmupEpochCount\) of the \(\DatasetSize\) samples; after warmup, training proceeds on the full dataset. From a systems standpoint, dynamically re-sorting multi-terabyte datasets epoch-by-epoch destroys sequential I/O read-ahead and stalls accelerators on storage latency. Production data loaders implement curricula by pre-sorting data offline into discrete difficulty-tiered shards or buckets. During training, workers stream shards sequentially from storage while maintaining local shuffle buffers within each tier, preserving high sequential storage bandwidth while maintaining batch diversity and gradient stability.

The pacing rule fixes one saving before any benchmark is run. Summed over the warmup, the loader serves \(\sum_{\EpochIndex=1}^{\WarmupEpochCount} \DatasetSize \cdot \EpochIndex/\WarmupEpochCount= \DatasetSize (\WarmupEpochCount+1)/2\) samples in place of \(\WarmupEpochCount\cdot \DatasetSize\), so the warmup epochs cost roughly half their full-data FLOPs. A curriculum can therefore save compute in two distinct ways, through cheaper warmup epochs and through any reduction in the total epochs needed to reach target quality, and a claimed saving must say which of the two it measured.

The difficulty scorer is a systems choice because it trades probe-compute overhead against ordering quality, as table 3 shows. Loss and confidence scoring gain better ordering at the cost of extra inference, heuristics avoid compute but require domain knowledge, and self-paced scoring moves adaptation into the training loop itself.

To see how curriculum savings translate to systems accounting, consider an illustrative workload where a linear-warmup curriculum reaches target accuracy in 115 epochs compared to 150 epochs for the standard shuffled baseline, an apparent 23.3 percent epoch reduction. Counting epochs alone, however, obscures the actual systems cost. Because linear pacing serves fewer samples during warmup, early epochs consume fewer FLOPs than post-warmup epochs; conversely, difficulty scoring and shard reordering introduce data-loading overhead that must be offset by the training speedup.

Alternative curriculum strategies diverge further in their computational demands. In a learned curriculum, an auxiliary network dynamically weights examples, down-weighting likely mislabeled instances, rather than enforcing a fixed difficulty schedule. Conversely, anti-curriculum presents the hardest examples first when boundary definition accelerates early convergence, while self-paced learning continuously updates sample difficulty using the current model’s training loss. Because optimal sample ordering depends on the architecture, optimizer, and label noise, curriculum gains cannot be assumed from nominal epoch reductions. They must be verified by measuring end-to-end wall-clock time at the target quality metric. Curricula also still assume that every candidate sample already has a label.

9.4.2 Active learning

Ordering a training stream helps only after labels exist, and in domains such as clinical imaging or specialist inspection each annotation requires expert time that can cost more than the training run itself. Active learning16 (Settles 2012; Ren et al. 2021), which Scaling with AI-assisted labeling introduced as a way to stretch a labeling budget, becomes a selection loop here. Rather than labeling an entire pool upfront, it shifts the optimization target from choosing which labeled samples to train on to choosing which unlabeled samples are worth paying an oracle to label.

16 Active learning: The “active” component is the learning algorithm itself selecting which unlabeled samples a human expert should label next. This reframes the problem from a computational optimization (training on given data) to a financial one: maximizing model improvement per dollar spent on expert labeling. Querying the most informative examples can substantially reduce labeling needs compared with random sampling, but the multiplier is task-, model-, and oracle-dependent.

Settles, Burr. 2012. Active Learning. Synthesis Lectures on Artificial Intelligence and Machine Learning. Springer Cham. https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-031-01560-1.
Ren, Pengzhen, Yun Xiao, Xiaojun Chang, Po-Yao Huang, Zhihui Li, Brij B. Gupta, Xiaojiang Chen, and Xin Wang. 2021. “A Survey of Deep Active Learning.” ACM Computing Surveys 54 (9): 1–40. https://doi.org/10.1145/3472291.

↩︎ The labeling budget, and what each label costs at scale, are established in Labeling economics.

Settles, Burr. 2009. Active Learning Literature Survey. Computer Sciences Technical Report 1648. University of Wisconsin-Madison.

Active learning replaces one-shot collection with an iterative loop of five stages (figure 6). The selection strategy queries the unlabeled pool based on model uncertainty and routes ambiguous instances to a human oracle. The loop then merges newly labeled data into the training set and retrains the model to update uncertainty scores over the remaining pool. The effectiveness of this loop depends on the query strategy used to select samples for annotation (Settles 2009, 2012; Ren et al. 2021). The simplest approach, uncertainty sampling, selects samples where the model is least confident, such as predictions near 0.5 probability for binary classification. Scoring with a single model is inexpensive, yet it can overconcentrate queries near one ambiguous region and miss parameters the model is confidently wrong about. Query-by-committee addresses that single-model blind spot by training multiple models and selecting samples where they disagree most, capturing epistemic uncertainty at the cost of ensemble inference. When practitioners can invest even more compute per candidate, expected model change selects the samples that would induce the largest gradient update if labeled, trading per-sample gradient evaluations for a more direct estimate of parameter impact. Any purely uncertainty- or gradient-driven rule can still cluster an entire acquisition batch inside one narrow pocket of feature space. Diversity sampling complements these methods by selecting samples that are dissimilar from currently labeled data, which spreads the annotation budget across the input distribution.

Active learning pays off most clearly in domains where every label requires scarce specialist judgment. When a ResNet-50-class image classifier moves from ImageNet to chest X-rays, for instance, every label requires a radiologist, and the model may be confident on common conditions but uncertain about rarer cases. By focusing human annotation on these ambiguous cases, active learning directs expensive expert time toward the examples most likely to move the decision boundary. Because annotation cost and turnaround in such domains dominate the compute needed to score candidates, routing only the examples the model is least sure about to the expert changes the economics of both labeling and per-epoch training work.

Napkin Math 9.3: The active learning ROI
Problem: A hospital team has 1 Million unlabeled scans, a $5/label specialist-label cost, and a $500,000 budget. How does uncertainty sampling change labeling cost and per-epoch training work relative to the budget-matched random baseline?

Scenario A: Naive Labeling

  • Cost: Labeling all 1 Million scans would cost $5,000,000 (10× over budget).
  • Budget-matched baseline: \(\$500,000 \div \$5/\text{label} = 100,000\) random scans.
  • Naive labeling outcome: Random selection does not guarantee rare-pathology coverage.

Scenario B: Active Learning

  • Strategy: Uncertainty sampling selects 50,000 scans for specialist labeling.
  • Cost: \(50,000 \times \$5/\text{label} = \$250,000\) (50 percent under budget).
  • Training work: \(100,000 \div 50,000 = 2×\) fewer examples per epoch than the budget-matched random baseline.
  • Active learning outcome: Matching 100,000 random scans requires empirical validation.

Systems insight: Against the budget-matched random baseline, selection saves \(\$500,000 - \$250,000 = \$250,000\); accuracy still requires empirical validation.

The notebook’s 2× label reduction is an assumption, and the size of that multiplier is what decides the outcome. In the illustrative learning curves of figure 7, uncertainty sampling reaches 90 percent accuracy with roughly 2,500 annotations while uniform random sampling needs about 10,000, a 4\(\times\) gap.

At a fixed price per label, label count and labeling dollars are the same axis, so the figure and notebook 9.3 differ only in the multiplier they assume. The notebook plans on the conservative 2×; the figure shows what a more favorable task would look like. Because the realized multiplier depends on the task, the model, and the query rule, a team budgets on the conservative value and measures the real one on its own data. Active learning also incurs scoring, acquisition, and repeated-retraining costs, so end-to-end savings can be smaller than the label-count gap. Beyond saving labeling cost, however, a suitable query rule concentrates review on uncertain, diverse, or failure-relevant examples that uniform sampling rarely surfaces.

Example 9.4: Hard negative mining in a smart doorbell
Scenario: A smart doorbell vision detector receives continuous video feeds dominated by empty backgrounds (easy negatives) and normal pedestrian poses.

Diagnosis: Random frame sampling can miss rare false positives such as statues, laundry piles, and human-shaped shadows. Active learning routes uncertain predictions (“Person: 51%”) and sampled production errors to human reviewers for targeted labeling.

Systems lesson: Hard-negative mining can improve labeling efficiency by spending review effort on suspected false positives and other difficult negatives. The query stream still needs diversity and coverage checks so confident but systematically wrong cases are not excluded.

Curriculum learning and active learning both choose among examples that already exist. When the pool itself lacks the cases a model needs (rare classes with too few examples, edge cases that never appear in the wild, records that privacy rules keep out of reach), no ordering or labeling policy can supply them. The remaining lever is to create them.

Self-Check: Question

  1. In curriculum learning, an engineer implements an easy-to-hard pacing schedule that controls the fraction of the sorted training pool available at each stage. What is the primary objective of this pacing strategy?

    1. To guide early optimization with more stable examples before gradually introducing harder ones
    2. To eliminate the need for backward passes during the first half of training
    3. To maximize GPU memory bandwidth utilization by sorting tensors strictly by length in bytes
    4. To replace human labelers with an automated oracle during the late stages of training

  2. An active learning pipeline chooses unlabeled examples for costly radiologist annotation. The team notices that simple uncertainty sampling repeatedly selects images from a single ambiguous artifact class, starving other disease categories. Which query strategy should they adopt to resolve this pathology?

    1. Least-confidence sampling, because it strictly selects the lowest top-1 probability prediction
    2. Diversity sampling or hybrid uncertainty-diversity sampling (such as BADGE), which balances uncertainty near decision boundaries with feature-space coverage
    3. Random undersampling of the entire unlabeled pool to reduce dataset size before scoring
    4. Uniform zero-shot pseudo-labeling without confidence thresholds

  3. Order the steps in an active learning closed-loop iteration: (1) Select top query samples via query strategy, (2) Acquire expert annotations from the oracle, (3) Score unlabeled pool using current model, (4) Retrain or update model on expanded dataset, (5) Add newly labeled samples to the training set.

See Answers →

9.5 Synthetic Data Generation

Real-world data collection encounters physical and legal boundaries: autonomous fleets cannot safely crash vehicles to acquire catastrophic collision trajectories, healthcare systems cannot transmit protected patient telemetry across institutional firewalls, and edge products must handle acoustic environments before any deployed fleet exists to record them. When the source corpus contains few or none of the cases deployment will face, neither static pruning nor dynamic selection can extract signal that is not there. Synthetic data generation, which Scaling acquisition introduced as one acquisition channel among several, becomes here a deliberate lever for constructing the training distribution directly. The strategy shifts from curation, choosing among existing records, to creation, making new inputs, perturbations, or supervisory targets.

9.5.1 Data augmentation

A vision model trained repeatedly on identical images can memorize their exact pixel layouts rather than the object class, yet storing multiple transformed copies of every image on disk would multiply the archive’s storage footprint. Data augmentation avoids that storage cost by transforming existing samples on the fly during ingestion; every ResNet-50 epoch on ImageNet sees freshly cropped, flipped, and color-shifted variants of the same images. Label-preserving transformations add diversity without expanding durable storage or acquiring new data, but they move cost into the training pipeline. On host CPU worker threads, decoding and spatial transformations can saturate the cores and starve the accelerator across the PCIe bus. Offloading them to accelerator kernels relieves the CPU, yet inputs still cross the host-to-device link, and the transformations consume HBM and compute cycles that would otherwise perform gradient updates.

For image data, transformations encode invariances required at deployment (Shorten and Khoshgoftaar 2019). Geometric operations (rotation, flipping, cropping, scaling) encourage viewpoint invariance when they preserve the label, while photometric adjustments (brightness, contrast, color jitter) simulate sensor noise and varying illumination. Stronger methods mask or mix images. Cutout17 applies random rectangular zero-masks to prevent reliance on localized features. MixUp (Zhang et al. 2018) constructs virtual samples by taking linear combinations of image pairs and their one-hot labels (\(\MixupSample= \MixupWeight \ModelInput_i + (1 - \MixupWeight) \ModelInput_j\), with mixing weight \(\MixupWeight\in [0, 1]\)). CutMix18 pastes rectangular patches from one image into another while blending labels proportionally to the patch area. Both mixing methods operate through linear combinations and block copies in device memory, so they add negligible FLOP overhead.

Shorten, Connor, and Taghi M. Khoshgoftaar. 2019. “A Survey on Image Data Augmentation for Deep Learning.” Journal of Big Data 6 (1): 1–48. https://doi.org/10.1186/s40537-019-0197-0.

17 Cutout: Randomly masks square regions of input images during training, forcing the model to recognize objects from partial information rather than relying on any single discriminative region (DeVries and Taylor 2017). Unlike dropout (which zeroes neurons in feature space), Cutout operates in input space. The original Cutout experiments produced 0.3–2.0 percentage-point gains across CIFAR-10/100 and SVHN with negligible compute overhead, making it a high-ICR augmentation technique when occlusion-style invariance matches the task: more information per sample at near-zero additional cost to the pipeline.

DeVries, Terrance, and Graham W. Taylor. 2017. “Improved Regularization of Convolutional Neural Networks with Cutout.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1708.04552.
Zhang, Hongyi, Moustapha Cisse, Yann N. Dauphin, and David Lopez-Paz. 2018. mixup: Beyond Empirical Risk Minimization. OpenReview.net.

18 CutMix: Replaces Cutout’s zeroed-out region with a patch from a different training image, mixing labels proportionally to patch area (30 percent of image A replaced by image B yields a 70/30 label split) (Yun et al. 2019). This addresses Cutout’s weakness: zeroed regions waste pixel information that could carry learning signal. The CutMix paper reports ImageNet top-1 improvements of 2.28 percentage points for ResNet-50 and 1.70 percentage points for ResNet-101, while also improving localization behavior. The method provides stronger regularization than either Cutout or MixUp alone with little additional data-pipeline cost.

Yun, Sangdoo, Dongyoon Han, Seong Joon Oh, Sanghyuk Chun, Junsuk Choe, and Youngjoon Yoo. 2019. “CutMix: Regularization Strategy to Train Strong Classifiers with Localizable Features.” 2019 IEEE/CVF International Conference on Computer Vision (ICCV), 6023–32. https://doi.org/10.1109/ICCV.2019.00612.

19 Back-translation: In the method described by Sennrich et al. (2016), target-language monolingual text is translated into the source language, and the synthetic source is paired with the unchanged target for machine-translation training. The systems trade-off is latency because each synthetic source requires translation-model inference. Pipelines can precompute these examples offline rather than generating them in the data loader.

Sennrich, Rico, Barry Haddow, and Alexandra Birch. 2016. “Improving Neural Machine Translation Models with Monolingual Data.” Proceedings of the 54th Annual Meeting of the Association for Computational Linguistics (Volume 1: Long Papers), 86–96. https://doi.org/10.18653/v1/P16-1009.

Text has no smooth geometric transformations, because tokens are discrete. Back-translation19 paraphrases by passing target-language text through a translation model to produce synthetic source sentences. Simpler transforms such as synonym substitution, token insertion, or deletion risk corrupting labels unless validated for the task.

Automated policy searches replace hand-tuned transformation schedules. AutoAugment20 uses reinforcement learning to search over operations, at a search cost of about 15,000 GPU-hours on ImageNet. RandAugment21 eliminates this search cost by parameterizing the policy with just two values: the number of transformations applied to each image and a single global distortion magnitude. Even the best augmentation schedule, however, can only perturb examples the dataset already contains.

20 AutoAugment: Treats augmentation policy design as a reinforcement learning search problem: a controller selects operations (rotate, translate, shear, equalize), their magnitudes, and application probabilities to maximize validation accuracy (Cubuk et al. 2019). The original ImageNet search used approximately 15,000 GPU-hours, motivating later methods that reduce the search space.

Cubuk, Ekin D., Barret Zoph, Dandelion Mané, Vijay Vasudevan, and Quoc V. Le. 2019. “AutoAugment: Learning Augmentation Strategies from Data.” 2019 IEEE/CVF Conference on Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition (CVPR), 113–23. https://doi.org/10.1109/cvpr.2019.00020.

21 RandAugment: Collapses AutoAugment’s policy search to two hyperparameters, a transformation count and a shared magnitude (Cubuk et al. 2020). That small search space matches or exceeds AutoAugment on the reported benchmarks at far lower search cost, making it practical when 15,000 GPU-hours of policy search cannot be justified.

Cubuk, Ekin D., Barret Zoph, Jonathon Shlens, and Quoc V. Le. 2020. “Randaugment: Practical Automated Data Augmentation with a Reduced Search Space.” 2020 IEEE/CVF Conference on Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition Workshops (CVPRW), 3008–17. https://doi.org/10.1109/cvprw50498.2020.00359.

9.5.2 Generative synthesis

Cropping or color-shifting existing images cannot invent a rare object class, an unseen weather condition, or a collision scenario that never occurred in the recorded dataset. When a pipeline needs examples that perturbations of existing records cannot supply, generative synthesis constructs new input-label pairs \((\ModelInput, \ExampleLabel)\) by sampling from generative models or physical simulators. The three main engines differ in cost per sample and in how realistic their outputs are:

  1. Generative Adversarial Networks (GANs) train a generator against a discriminator. Once trained, feed-forward generator inference requires a single forward pass, providing low latency and high sample throughput. However, GAN training is vulnerable to mode collapse, in which the generator covers only part of the data distribution and loses the tail variety needed for edge-case coverage.
  2. Diffusion models generate samples by iteratively removing noise over many sequential steps. Latent diffusion systems such as Stable Diffusion22 enable precise semantic conditioning from text prompts while operating in a compressed latent space. A sampling recipe with 20 to 50 denoising steps runs the backbone that many times per generated batch, so offline generation is often preferable.
  3. Physical simulation engines (such as CARLA for autonomous driving or Isaac Sim and Unity for robotics) generate data without neural inference, by running physics and rendering pipelines. They produce exact ground-truth annotations (3D bounding boxes, depth maps, velocity vectors) from internal state at no labeling cost, and the bottleneck shifts to host CPU physics and graphics rendering throughput.

22 Stable Diffusion: Performs iterative denoising in a compressed latent space, enabling targeted text-to-image examples (Rombach et al. 2022). Its iterative generation is substantially more compute-intensive than geometric transforms, so it fits cases where novelty matters more than per-sample throughput.

Rombach, Robin, Andreas Blattmann, Dominik Lorenz, Patrick Esser, and Bjorn Ommer. 2022. “High-Resolution Image Synthesis with Latent Diffusion Models.” 2022 IEEE/CVF Conference on Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition (CVPR), 10674–85. https://doi.org/10.1109/cvpr52688.2022.01042.

Regardless of the generative engine, synthetic samples inevitably diverge from the statistical distributions of real deployment environments. That divergence determines whether synthetic training transfers to production or fails on the first real-world input.

9.5.3 Bridging the domain gap

A model trained purely on synthetic data can reach high validation accuracy in the simulator while failing in production because it latched onto rendering or generator artifacts that do not exist in the real world. This domain gap23 is the statistical shift between the synthetic training distribution and production deployment data. Synthetic and real feature densities can sit at different locations in feature space, misaligning the decision boundaries learned from each (figure 8). The marginal densities alone do not determine either decision boundary; class-conditional structure and real-data evaluation are needed to establish how the shift affects classification.

23 Domain gap: The statistical divergence between synthetic and real data distributions, measurable with metrics such as maximum mean discrepancy (MMD) or Frechet Inception Distance (FID). For ML systems, the gap becomes training-serving skew: a simulator-trained model can validate well on synthetic data while failing silently on real deployment data.

Two complementary strategies address this distribution mismatch. Domain randomization24 takes an aggressive approach: rather than trying to match the real world precisely, it trains on varied synthetic data by randomizing lighting, textures, backgrounds, and camera parameters during generation. The aim is to make deployment conditions fall within the variation seen during training, though coverage is never guaranteed by randomization alone. The method is effective in robotics and autonomous-driving settings when the simulator spans the relevant physical and visual factors and the result is validated on real data.

24 Domain randomization: Makes synthetic data deliberately varied by randomizing textures, colors, lighting, and physical properties. The goal is not photorealism; it is enough variation to improve coverage of deployment conditions, shifting the cost bottleneck from rendering fidelity to coverage.

Domain adaptation takes the opposite approach by explicitly aligning synthetic and real distributions. Feature alignment methods train on synthetic data while simultaneously minimizing the distance between synthetic and real feature distributions, often using adversarial training to learn domain-invariant representations. Fine-tuning offers a simpler path: pretrain on abundant synthetic data to learn general features, then fine-tune on a small real dataset to adapt to deployment conditions. Self-training combines these ideas by using a synthetic-trained model to pseudo-label real unlabeled data, then retraining on the combined labeled set. In practice, mixing synthetic and real data provides a real-data anchor while retaining some synthetic coverage. Moving the mix from all-synthetic toward all-real trades a larger domain gap for a higher collection cost. The best ratio depends on the domain, simulator fidelity, model family, and validation protocol rather than on any universal recipe.

Augmentation, noise injection, and simulation can grow a small seed set into a training corpus. The keyword spotter’s data budget, including how much synthetic augmentation saves against recording real utterances, is worked through in KWS design space. The same creation strategy becomes riskier when samples come from learned generators. In recursive training, model collapse25 can amplify errors and reduce diversity over generations. Synthetic data therefore requires provenance, mixture controls, and validation against real deployment data rather than an unverified assumption that more generated samples help. When the input examples themselves are already on disk, a trained model can instead enrich their labels.

25 Model collapse: Formally analyzed by Shumailov et al. (2024), this phenomenon can arise when errors and missing tail coverage compound during recursive training. Tail distributions contain rare but important patterns that appear infrequently in training data. When generation \(\ModelGenerationIndex+1\) trains on output from generation \(\ModelGenerationIndex\) under these conditions, successive generations can further compress the tails, producing increasingly homogeneous data. The degradation can be rapid in recursive training settings, which is why training on unanchored synthetic outputs risks catastrophic collapse of tail diversity.

Shumailov, Ilia, Zakhar Shumaylov, Yiren Zhao, Nicolas Papernot, Ross Anderson, and Yarin Gal. 2024. “AI Models Collapse When Trained on Recursively Generated Data.” Nature 631 (8022): 755–59. https://doi.org/10.1038/s41586-024-07566-y.

9.5.4 Knowledge distillation

A hard one-hot label such as \([1, 0, 0]\) tells a classifier which class is right, but discards everything a stronger model knows about which wrong classes look similar. Where augmentation (section 9.5.1) and generative synthesis (section 9.5.2) create new inputs, knowledge distillation26 (Hinton et al. 2015; Gou et al. 2021) creates richer targets for inputs the corpus already holds. A larger trained model (the teacher) labels each training example with its full probability distribution over classes, such as \([0.70, 0.20, 0.10]\) in place of \([1, 0, 0]\). A compact student network then trains against those soft targets, smoothed by a temperature parameter \(\DistillationTemperature\). Because each soft target records which wrong classes the teacher found plausible, every example carries more supervision than its one-hot label. Distillation mathematics develops distillation as a compression technique, including its loss and temperature mechanics; as a data lever, its cost lies in generating, storing, and reading those targets.

26 Knowledge distillation: Soft teacher probabilities reveal structural similarities across classes that one-hot encodings omit, and a temperature parameter controls how strongly those probabilities are smoothed. Distillation introduces teacher inference costs that must be justified by improved student sample efficiency or accuracy.

Hinton, Geoffrey, Oriol Vinyals, and Jeff Dean. 2015. “Distilling the Knowledge in a Neural Network.” arXiv Preprint.
Gou, Jianping, Baosheng Yu, Stephen J. Maybank, and Dacheng Tao. 2021. “Knowledge Distillation: A Survey.” International Journal of Computer Vision 129 (6): 1789–819. https://doi.org/10.1007/s11263-021-01453-z.

Distillation trades accelerator HBM capacity against storage I/O bandwidth:

  • Online distillation: The teacher model resides directly in accelerator memory alongside the student model during training. The system computes teacher forward passes concurrently with student execution, eliminating persistent storage overhead for soft targets. However, co-locating an overparameterized teacher consumes accelerator memory and arithmetic throughput, restricting the student’s maximum per-accelerator batch size and increasing step latency.
  • Offline distillation: The teacher generates predictions across the corpus during an offline precomputation pass, storing dense logit vectors to disk. This frees accelerator memory entirely for student training, but expands dataset storage volume and input pipeline bandwidth. For a classification vocabulary of \(\ClassCount\) classes, storing uncompressed float32 logits increases the per-sample label footprint from a single integer index (\(\lceil \log_2 \ClassCount \rceil\) bits) to \(4\ClassCount\) bytes, an expansion of more than three orders of magnitude once \(\ClassCount\) reaches the thousands.

For ResNet-50 on ImageNet the offline footprint is concrete. Storing FP32 logits for 1,000 classes across every training image takes 5.1 GB, against 2.6 MB for the integer labels they replace, a 2,000× expansion that every epoch must read back through the input pipeline. That storage and read traffic, rather than the loss function, is what data selection must budget for. Generation and distillation both supply labeled signal the corpus lacks; a complementary route reduces how many labels training needs in the first place, by extracting supervision from the unlabeled data the corpus already holds.

Self-Check: Question

  1. What causes the phenomenon of ‘model collapse’ when generative models are trained recursively on synthetic data generated by earlier model iterations?

    1. GPU memory fragmentation caused by variable-length synthetic sequences during distributed training
    2. Overfitting to floating-point rounding errors during fp16 mixed-precision matrix multiplication
    3. A failure of the I/O storage subsystem to deliver synthetic batches at line rate
    4. Systematic underrepresentation and erosion of tail patterns across generations, so recursive retraining compounds missing or distorted rare cases

  2. A team distills a ResNet-50 student on ImageNet and chooses offline distillation, precomputing the teacher’s FP32 logits for every training image instead of keeping the teacher in accelerator memory. Which cost does this choice move onto the data path?

    1. Every epoch must read per-class logit vectors in place of single integer labels, multiplying the label bytes the storage tier and input pipeline must deliver
    2. Every training step must run a teacher forward pass beside the student, restricting the student’s per-accelerator batch size
    3. The student must be trained with a lower learning rate, because soft targets produce larger gradients than one-hot labels
    4. The teacher must be retrained once per student epoch, so preparation cost grows linearly with the number of epochs

  3. Why is training exclusively on \(100\%\) synthetic data from a simulation engine often suboptimal for real-world deployment, and how does synthetic-to-real data mixing bridge this gap?

  4. True or False: Using a high-capacity diffusion model or state-of-the-art LLM to generate synthetic training data guarantees that the resulting training set will be completely free of distribution shift relative to the real deployment environment.

9.6 Learning from Unlabeled Data

A hospital archive holding tens of thousands of unannotated chest X-rays or a language-modeling team holding terabytes of raw web text already has its inputs on disk; the bottleneck is that active learning still pays an expert for every example it selects and synthesis still pays for every example it creates. Learning from unlabeled data attacks that label bottleneck from the other side. Semi-supervised learning lets a model trained on a small labeled seed supply provisional labels for the rest of the pool. Self-supervised learning removes task labels from pretraining altogether, and foundation models spread the resulting pretraining cost across many downstream tasks. Each approach trades additional training compute for fewer labels, so each is judged by total cost at matched quality rather than by label count alone.

9.6.1 Semi-supervised learning

In the chest X-ray adaptation of section 9.4.2, a hospital may hold 50,000 scans of which only 500 have been reviewed and labeled27 by radiologists, a labeling rate of 1 percent. Semi-supervised learning puts the remaining 49,500 images to work by shifting the trade-off from human annotation to accelerator compute: it uses the small labeled subset to anchor training on the larger unlabeled pool, extracting supervisory signals from unannotated inputs. Models trained this way can approach fully supervised accuracy with far fewer manual labels, provided the unlabeled pool matches the target distribution and the result is validated on labeled data.

27 Illustrative clinical labeling economics: The review-rate and hourly-rate ranges are scenario assumptions rather than reported measurements. Under those inputs, labeling 500 scans requires 7–10 hours and costs $1,000–3,000; labeling all 50,000 costs $94,000–300,000. The arithmetic illustrates why semi-supervised learning can be attractive when expert labels are expensive, but actual review time, adjudication, prevalence, and validation requirements must be measured.

Unlabeled data, although it cannot teach the mapping from inputs to outputs, contains structural information about the input distribution \(\InputDistribution\) that can constrain the hypothesis space. Under smoothness and cluster assumptions, a decision boundary that cuts through a dense region assigns different labels to nearby inputs and is less plausible than one in a low-density region. Semi-supervised methods exploit these assumptions, which must be checked against the target task.

Three techniques implement this distribution-regularization insight. Pseudo-labeling28 trains on the labeled subset, generates model predictions on the unlabeled pool, and incorporates samples exceeding a confidence threshold as synthetic ground truth for subsequent retraining. The confidence threshold governs a systems trade-off: setting it too low admits label noise that degrades learning, while setting it too high discards informative examples and wastes the unlabeled pool. Confident mistakes can survive either threshold, so confidence must be checked against labeled validation evidence rather than treated as proof of correctness.

28 Pseudo-labeling: Uses a trained model’s own confident predictions as ground-truth labels for unlabeled data; the technique’s effectiveness depends on a virtuous cycle in which accurate predictions on easy unlabeled examples expand the training set and improve the model, which then labels harder examples accurately. The failure mode is equally self-reinforcing, because incorrect pseudo-labels feed the model’s own misclassifications back into training through confirmation bias.

29 Consistency regularization: The method rests on a task-dependent smoothness assumption, that two inputs \(\ModelInput_1\) and \(\ModelInput_2\) related by a label-preserving perturbation should receive the same prediction. It is conceptually distinct from data augmentation (which creates more training examples) because it explicitly enforces prediction consistency as a loss term, even for unlabeled data where the “correct” label is unknown. The systems consequence is additional computation for generating and comparing predictions under multiple perturbations, a trade-off that can favor compute-rich, label-poor settings.

30 FixMatch: The confidence gate is strict; in the original experiments a pseudo-label is kept only when the prediction on the weak view exceeds 0.95 (Sohn et al. 2020). Every unlabeled image still costs a forward pass on its weak view whether or not it passes the gate, part of the extra training compute that buys the reduction in manual labels.

Sohn, Kihyuk, David Berthelot, Nicholas Carlini, Zizhao Zhang, Han Zhang, Colin A Raffel, Ekin Dogus Cubuk, Alexey Kurakin, and Chun-Liang Li. 2020. “FixMatch: Simplifying Semi-Supervised Learning with Consistency and Confidence.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 33: 596–608.

Consistency regularization29 enforces that the model produce invariant predictions across perturbed versions of the same input. When transformations such as cropping, rotation, or color shifts preserve the task label, the loss function penalizes prediction divergence between two stochastic views of an unlabeled sample. Hybrid approaches like FixMatch30 unify both mechanisms: they generate a pseudo-label on a weakly augmented view when prediction confidence exceeds a threshold, then train the model to predict that label on a strongly augmented variant of the same input.

Label propagation frames semi-supervised learning as graph diffusion: it constructs an affinity graph over sample representations and propagates labels from labeled nodes to unlabeled neighbors. This approach works well when representations form well-separated clusters, though constructing and querying the similarity graph across large datasets introduces substantial memory and nearest-neighbor search overhead.

Example 9.5: FixMatch on CIFAR-10
Scenario: A semi-supervised image classification pipeline trains on CIFAR-10 with only 250 labeled samples (25 per class) alongside unlabeled data (Sohn et al. 2020).

Diagnosis: The fully labeled CIFAR-10 training set contains 50K labels. FixMatch generates high-confidence pseudo-labels (>0.95 threshold) on weakly augmented unlabeled images and enforces consistency on strongly augmented variants, reaching 94.9 percent accuracy in the cited experiment.

Systems lesson: Semi-supervised learning trades additional training compute for fewer manual labels. In an illustrative cost scenario at $1 per label, a supervised baseline with 4,000 labels costs $4,000 in labels plus $50 of compute, or $4,050. FixMatch at 250 labels costs $250 in labels but 5× the compute, $250, for $500. Preparation expense falls by 8.1×; the end-to-end saving depends on the real unlabeled-data and compute costs.

The objective of semi-supervised learning is lower total labeling and compute cost at matched quality, which can justify a lower ICR when annotation savings exceed the added training expense. Neither the label reduction nor the compute increase carries over unchanged from one task to another; table 4 shows how far the label count fell on one benchmark.

The efficiency column counts labels only, relative to the full labeled set. It does not claim that FixMatch matches a fully supervised model, which would have to be compared at equal accuracy. These efficiency gains also depend on benign distribution assumptions. Semi-supervised learning assumes that unlabeled data comes from the same distribution as labeled data. It struggles when unlabeled data contains out-of-distribution samples (the model confidently mislabels them), when class imbalance is severe (pseudo-labels amplify majority class bias), or when the labeled set does not cover all classes (preventing label propagation for unseen classes). A held-out set with true labels is the check that catches such a mismatch.

Each technique can reduce dependence on task-specific labels, but none eliminates the need for labeled evaluation. The progression raises a related possibility, that task-specific labels may not be necessary for pretraining. The structure of data itself (cat images resemble other cat images, and coherent sentences follow grammatical patterns) may provide a supervision signal, while downstream quality still requires task-aligned validation.

9.6.2 Self-supervised learning

GPT was trained to predict the next token in a sequence. BERT was trained to fill in masked tokens. Neither pretraining objective required task-specific human labels. Self-supervised learning31 (SSL) generalizes this insight: by designing pretext tasks that derive supervision from the data’s structure, models can learn reusable representations from unlabeled data at scale. Where active and semi-supervised learning reduce the demand for labels, SSL changes the accounting by making unlabeled structure the pretraining signal. Within the intervention map of figure 1, SSL sits beside the three levers and extends the usable pool; the question shifts from which labeled samples to train on to how unlabeled samples can support pretraining. It responds to one aspect of the data wall introduced in section 9.1 by broadening what counts as a training signal, while leaving corpus quality, licensing, coverage, and labeled evaluation requirements intact.

31 Self-supervised learning: The pretext task, such as next-token or masked-token prediction, provides a supervisory signal derived from the data itself. This reduces dependence on task-specific human labels but can require substantial pretraining compute. Amortization depends on how broadly the resulting model is reused.

Labels are one form of supervision; a pretext task can also derive a learning signal from the structure of the data, as table 5 summarizes.

Pretext tasks generate supervision signals automatically. Masked-token objectives can encourage models to learn grammatical and semantic structure; next-token prediction teaches continuation structure; and contrastive image objectives encourage visual features that are invariant to selected transformations. These approaches build on convolutional neural network and transformer families, but the systems accounting matters: self-supervision removes task-specific annotation from the pretraining gate rather than removing data cost. Pretraining can begin on unlabeled corpora without waiting for example-level labels, but collection, filtering, licensing, storage, and processing remain. If the representation transfers, one pretraining run serves many tasks and each task needs far fewer labels; task-specific adaptation and evaluation still carry their own data requirements.

When its pretraining cost is reused across tasks, SSL supports the foundation model paradigm32 (Bommasani et al. 2021). The core data selection principles (coreset selection, curriculum learning, and active learning) remain essential within this paradigm. Pretraining corpus curation applies deduplication and quality filtering at web scale, while downstream selection dictates which fine-tuning examples maximize task-specific transfer.

32 Foundation model: The name emphasizes that these models serve as a shared base for many downstream tasks, but this creates a single point of failure. Defects in the foundation model’s pretraining data (biases, factual errors, memorized private content) can propagate to multiple applications built upon it, depending on transfer and downstream adaptation. From a systems perspective, this homogenization risk means that data selection quality during pretraining has an outsized blast radius: a curation error that would affect one task in the train-from-scratch paradigm can affect many downstream deployments.

Bommasani, Rishi, Drew A. Hudson, Ehsan Adeli, Russ Altman, Simran Arora, Sydney von Arx, Michael S. Bernstein, et al. 2021. “On the Opportunities and Risks of Foundation Models.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2108.07258.

One pretraining corpus defect can affect many downstream tasks.

Chen, Ting, Simon Kornblith, Mohammad Norouzi, and Geoffrey Hinton. 2020. “A Simple Framework for Contrastive Learning of Visual Representations.” Proceedings of the 37th International Conference on Machine Learning, ICML’20, vol. 119: 1597–607.
He, Kaiming, Haoqi Fan, Yuxin Wu, Saining Xie, and Ross Girshick. 2020. “Momentum Contrast for Unsupervised Visual Representation Learning.” 2020 IEEE/CVF Conference on Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition (CVPR), 9726–35. https://doi.org/10.1109/cvpr42600.2020.00975.

Pretext objectives differ in what they stress on the machine. Contrastive learning trains a model to map two augmented views of the same image to nearby representations while pushing the representations of different images apart. Those other images are the negatives, and the objective learns better with more of them. SimCLR (Chen et al. 2020) draws its negatives from the rest of the mini-batch, with batches of up to 8,192 examples, so the negative count is paid for in activation memory and, across accelerators, in all-gather traffic that collects every device’s representations. Momentum Contrast (MoCo) (He et al. 2020) instead keeps recent representations in a queue produced by a slowly updated copy of the encoder (the momentum encoder), which decouples the number of negatives from batch size. Masked autoencoders split an image into small square patches, hide most of them, and train the model to reconstruct what is hidden; because the encoder sees only the visible patches, it spends far less memory and compute per image. Autoregressive pretraining, by contrast, scales with tokens. For data selection, the common point is that pretraining turns raw structure into reusable signal that multiplies the value of scarce labels. Because the pretraining run itself is expensive, however, whether self-supervised pretraining pays off depends on how many downstream tasks reuse it.

9.6.3 Foundation-model amortization

Pretraining a self-supervised model on a massive unlabeled corpus can consume thousands of accelerator hours, an upfront bill that rarely pays off if the resulting checkpoint serves only a single downstream classifier. Cost amortization makes that investment economical by splitting work into two phases: an expensive pretraining pass run once on unlabeled data, and lightweight task-specific adaptations, usually fine-tuning, reused across many applications (table 6).

↩︎ Fine-tuning’s memory and compute cost on one accelerator, set by which parameters it updates, is established in Fine-tuning memory on one accelerator.

Take a company building 10 specialized classifiers. Under the scenario assumptions, each task trained from scratch needs 100,000 labels at $1 per label, for $1M across all tasks and a modeled compute burden of 10,000 GPU-hours. The fine-tuning scenario instead pays 10,000 GPU-hours once, then assigns 1,000 labels and 50 GPU-hours of compute to each task, bringing the modeled fine-tuning label bill across all 10 tasks to $10K. Assuming both approaches reach the same target quality on every downstream task, labeling cost drops by 100× and per-task marginal compute by 20× as direct consequences of the chosen scenario values. Total compute becomes favorable only after enough downstream tasks amortize pretraining.

The compute-only crossover occurs when enough downstream tasks share the pretraining cost, provided the representations transfer at the required quality. A dollar crossover also depends on labeling savings, compute prices, and other costs that differ between the approaches. The side-by-side bar charts in figure 9 contrast the cost structures across ten downstream tasks. Training from scratch expends a constant 1,000 GPU-hours per task (totaling 10,000 GPU-hours). The foundation model approach absorbs a 10,000 GPU-hour pretraining bar upfront but collapses subsequent task fine-tuning to 50 GPU-hours per bar.

Every lever is now in view: pruning, reordering, labeling, creating, and learning without labels. Each has been judged so far by the work it removes; the next question is whether the machine can run it without giving that saving back.

Self-Check: Question

  1. Explain the mechanism of confirmation bias in semi-supervised pseudo-labeling, and specify how confidence thresholding mitigates it.

  2. True or False: Consistency regularization methods such as FixMatch assume that label-preserving perturbations of an input should not change the model’s predicted class distribution much.

  3. In the economics of foundation models, an organization invests \(C_{\text{pretrain}} = 10{,}000\) GPU-hours in self-supervised pretraining. Each downstream task fine-tuning costs \(C_{\text{finetune}} = 50\) GPU-hours. Training each task from scratch would cost \(C_{\text{scratch}} = 1{,}000\) GPU-hours. What is the minimum number of downstream tasks \(N^*\) required to break even on the pretraining investment?

    1. \(N^* = 5\) downstream tasks
    2. \(N^* = 8\) downstream tasks
    3. \(N^* = 11\) downstream tasks (\(10{,}000 + 11 \times 50 = 10{,}550 < 11 \times 1{,}000 = 11{,}000\))
    4. \(N^* = 50\) downstream tasks

  4. How does the MoCo (Momentum Contrast) framework reduce the hardware and memory constraints of contrastive self-supervised learning compared to naive SimCLR?

    1. By replacing convolutional backbones with rule-based lookup tables to avoid backpropagation
    2. By requiring fully supervised class labels to filter out false negative pairs
    3. By using a dynamic queue of negative keys and a slowly updating momentum encoder, decoupling negative dictionary size from mini-batch size
    4. By computing exact pairwise Jaccard similarities on raw byte sequences rather than latent embeddings

  5. What is the systems-level ‘homogenization risk’ (or blast radius) of pretraining a shared foundation model on an uncurated dataset?

  6. True or False: Because self-supervised pretraining learns general representations from unlabeled data, it completely eliminates the need for data selection or quality filtering during the pretraining stage.

9.7 Selection Engineering

A naive active learning loop that scans the entire dataset every epoch to select the “best” samples can turn a compute-bound training job into an I/O-bound bottleneck. Selection engineering is the work of making a chosen technique deliver its promised speedup on real hardware and real data pipelines. The pipeline must keep scoring cheap, reads sequential, and the accelerator fed.

The machine sets the scale for every one of those patterns. One ImageNet epoch of ResNet-50 on one H100 passes through four stages: images are read from local storage, decoded and augmented on host CPUs, copied across PCIe into accelerator memory, and consumed by the accelerator’s arithmetic. Pruning shrinks the last stage directly; whether the other stages shrink, stay the same, or grow depends on how the selection is engineered (figure 10).

Napkin Math 9.4: One pruned ResNet-50 epoch on the H100
Problem: ResNet-50 trains on the 1.28 million ImageNet training images for 90 epochs on one H100, and a coreset keeps 50 percent of the images. How much accelerator time and energy does the coreset save once its scoring pass is paid?

Compute: Each image costs 24.6 GFLOP forward and backward, so a full epoch is 3.2 × 10¹⁶ FLOPs. At the 25 percent of peak that ResNet-50 sustains in training (Data pipeline), the H100 finishes it in about 126 seconds, which at its 700 W board power is about 88 kJ. The pruned epoch takes about 63 seconds and 44 kJ.

Selection: An EL2N-style scoring pass is one forward pass over the full pool, a third of an epoch’s arithmetic, about 42 seconds and 29 kJ, paid once. Over 90 epochs the coreset saves about 4 MJ and 2 hours of accelerator time, 135× the scoring energy.

Data path: To stay compute-bound, the accelerator must receive about 10,100 images per second. Reading the 148 GB ImageNet archive from local NVMe at 7 GB/s takes about 21 seconds per epoch, and copying the decoded 8-bit tensors (193 GB) across PCIe at 64 GB/s takes about 3 seconds, both inside even the pruned epoch. The stage that must keep up is the host CPU, which has to decode and augment 10,100 JPEG images every second. The training chapter’s host supplies only about 5,250 per accelerator, so an epoch actually takes about 244 seconds until decode capacity grows; the seconds below are the compute-bound figures selection can claim once the host keeps up.

Systems insight: Pruning halves the Data factor (\(\text{Samples/Task}\)), cutting total arithmetic \(\Operations\) and accelerator energy in half. It halves the storage read (\(\DataVolume/\Bandwidth\)), however, only if the retained subset is written out as its own shards. Streaming the full archive and discarding half leaves storage traffic unchanged. The seconds come from FLOPs, and whether they are realized depends on the slowest stage of the data path, which for ResNet-50 is often host decoding (Pipeline stages and accelerator bubbles).

Each lever also adds memory or storage of its own and compute or I/O of its own, and those costs land on different stages of the same data path (table 7).

Those added costs surface in four places (scoring, storage reads, host preparation, and coordination across workers), and each has an engineering pattern that keeps it off the critical path.

9.7.1 The selection bottleneck

Evaluating a selection function \(\SelectionScoreFunction(\ModelInput)\) over a large candidate pool before forming every batch can easily cost more compute than training on the unselected data. While a conventional loader follows a predetermined sampling plan, active learning and adaptive curricula score candidates dynamically during training, introducing selection latency. In the ResNet-50 example of section 9.2.6, one static scoring pass with the target model costs a third of an epoch and is paid once. Rescoring the full ImageNet pool before every one of the 100 epochs instead adds 70 minutes of forward passes to 21 minutes of subset training, more than three times the training it serves. The rescoring cuts the saving from 90 percent to 57 percent. The selection inequality (equation 2) still holds, but scoring has become the largest term, and a heavier scorer or a smaller retained fraction would break it.

Engineering pipelines keep selection latency off the critical path through proxy models, cached embeddings, and indexed retrieval. A smaller proxy model curtails scoring time provided its sample ranking transfers accurately to the target architecture, and running the scoring pass in reduced numerical precision raises arithmetic throughput and cuts memory traffic when ranking fidelity permits. For similarity-based or diversity-based selection, vector indices such as FAISS33 replace expensive corpus-wide scans with approximate nearest-neighbor retrieval over precomputed embeddings. These techniques decouple selection evaluation from primary model training and shrink \(\SelectionTime\), but even zero-cost scoring can still slow down training if fetching the selected indices stalls the storage layer.

33 FAISS (Facebook AI Similarity Search): Provides GPU-accelerated similarity search using exact, approximate, and compressed-domain index designs (Johnson et al. 2019); Johnson, Douze, and Jegou report billion-vector graph construction on multiple GPUs, showing why vector-index infrastructure matters for web-scale selection. For data selection pipelines, FAISS-style indexing supports \(\NeighborCount\)-nearest-neighbor retrieval for coreset selection, embedding-based deduplication, and stratified clustering for balanced sampling. Without this infrastructure, embedding-based selection would often fall back to expensive full-corpus scans.

Johnson, Jeff, Matthijs Douze, and Hervé Jégou. 2019. “Billion-Scale Similarity Search with GPUs.” IEEE Transactions on Big Data 7 (3): 535–47. https://doi.org/10.1109/tbdata.2019.2921572.

9.7.2 The random-access penalty

Selecting a sparse subset of examples scattered across a dataset turns large sequential shard transfers into thousands of nonsequential reads, forfeiting hardware readahead, OS page caching, and bulk controller prefetching. Training already pays for shuffling through the access factor \(\StorageAccessFactor\) that scales effective storage throughput in the data-pipeline model of Core components, and selection pushes that factor lower by design because the retained records no longer sit contiguously on disk. Because disk heads must physically seek and flash controllers must execute random block lookups, two subsets of equal size can differ sharply in wall-clock training time according to their access locality. Table 8 quantifies this small-read penalty across storage tiers for 4 KB transfers.

↩︎ Random access is measured in IOPS and streaming access in sequential throughput, the two storage metrics established in Storage system options.

Data loaders (whose general mechanism Data loading develops) avoid the problem by packing examples into large shards and shuffling inside a memory buffer. Sharded dataset formats, such as WebDataset and FFCV, pack thousands of individual examples into contiguous, sequentially readable archive chunks. Rather than fetching scattered individual samples from disk, the loader streams large sequential shards into host memory and populates a shuffle buffer. The training loop samples pseudo-randomly from this in-memory buffer, preserving peak storage read bandwidth while satisfying the statistical requirement of randomized mini-batches.

Converting a logical set of selected indices into sequential physical reads requires choosing between two storage strategies. For static pruning (such as a fixed coreset or a deduplicated corpus), the pipeline can materialize the subset offline by repacking the retained records into new, contiguous shards. This step incurs a one-time sequential read and write cost alongside additional disk storage, but ensures that every subsequent training epoch transfers only retained bytes from storage. Conversely, when the selection policy updates dynamically across training iterations or active-learning rounds, repeatedly repacking multi-terabyte shards on disk would choke the filesystem on write bandwidth. Data loaders instead stream the original, full-volume shards sequentially into host memory and discard unselected records before populating the shuffle buffer. This online filtering preserves sequential read throughput and eliminates seek penalties, but it leaves physical storage traffic unchanged: the host continues to read 100 percent of the corpus bytes from disk even when accelerators train on only a 10 percent subset. Systems engineers must count this ongoing read traffic on storage-bound nodes and include offline materialization costs when evaluating the selection inequality. Even after storage reads are streaming at full speed, host-side decoding and augmentation can still starve the accelerator.

Checkpoint 9.2: Selection overhead and storage access

Equation checks

Systems implications

9.7.3 Data echoing

Even with sequential reads, the host CPU often cannot decode and augment samples fast enough. Heavy data transformations, such as 3D rotations, MixUp, JPEG decoding, or on-the-fly generative synthesis, demand substantial host processing per sample. When the host pipeline produces batches slower than the accelerator executes forward and backward passes, the accelerator idles, starved for input; this is the feeding problem reappearing inside the selection loop. The resulting collapse in accelerator utilization extends training wall-clock time, neutralizing the efficiency gains achieved by pruning the dataset.

↩︎ Accelerator bubbles, which open when the data-loading stage cannot keep pace with the accelerator, are established in Pipeline stages and accelerator bubbles.

34 Data echoing: The key subtlety is where in the pipeline to insert the echo point. Echoing before augmentation (upstream echoing) can apply different random augmentations to each repetition, while echoing after augmentation feeds identical tensors to the accelerator. Useful echo factors and realized speedups depend on the workload, batch size, insertion point, and shuffling policy (Choi et al. 2019).

Choi, Dami, Alexandre Passos, Christopher J. Shallue, and George E. Dahl. 2019. “Faster Neural Network Training with Data Echoing.” arXiv Preprint, ahead of print. https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.1907.05550.

Data echoing34 (Choi et al. 2019) reuses data multiple times before fetching new samples, trading freshness for accelerator utilization when the input pipeline is slower than training. Echoing before randomized augmentation can produce different transformed inputs on each repetition, while echoing later in the pipeline may repeat identical tensors.

The optimal echo factor depends on the ratio \(\InputPipelineRatio\) of upstream processing time to downstream training time, where \(\HostPreparationTime\) is the time the data pipeline needs to produce a batch and \(\AcceleratorBatchTime\) is the time the accelerator needs to train on it: \[ \InputPipelineRatio= \frac{\HostPreparationTime}{\AcceleratorBatchTime} \]

In this two-stage rate model, \(\InputPipelineRatio> 1\) means the data pipeline takes longer to prepare a fresh batch than the accelerator takes to train on it once. When the loader repeats each prepared batch \(\EchoFactor\) times, the accelerator spends \(\EchoFactor \cdot \AcceleratorBatchTime\) training on the current batch while the host pipeline prepares the next fresh batch in parallel over time \(\HostPreparationTime\). An echo factor \(\EchoFactor< \InputPipelineRatio\) therefore recovers part of the idle gap, while \(\EchoFactor\ge \InputPipelineRatio\) makes \(\EchoFactor \cdot \AcceleratorBatchTime \ge \HostPreparationTime\) so the next batch is ready before the accelerator finishes the current one, removing input waiting entirely provided the repeated samples remain statistically productive. Raising \(\EchoFactor\) beyond \(\InputPipelineRatio\) adds no further throughput and only reduces gradient diversity. When \(\InputPipelineRatio< 1\), the input pipeline already outruns the accelerator, so echoing has nothing to recover.

Echoing raises accelerator utilization until \(\EchoFactor\) reaches \(\InputPipelineRatio\); beyond it, nothing is gained.

Napkin Math 9.5: Worked example: Data echoing ROI
Scenario: Training ResNet-50 on ImageNet on one H100 fed by the training chapter’s host, where host preparation is the slow stage (Data pipeline).

Rates:

  • Data pipeline throughput: 5,250 images/s (the host cores available to one accelerator, each reading, decoding, and augmenting about 375 images/s)
  • Accelerator training throughput: 10,100 images/s (forward and backward pass at the compute-bound rate of notebook 9.4)
  • Ratio \(\InputPipelineRatio= \HostPreparationTime / \AcceleratorBatchTime\) = (1/5,250 images/s) / (1/10,100 images/s) = 10,100 images/s/5,250 images/s ≈ 1.92 (accelerator waiting 48.0 percent of the time)

Without echoing:

  • Effective throughput: 5,250 images/s (limited by data pipeline)
  • Training time for 90 epochs: \(90 \times 1.28\text{M}\) / 5,250 images/s = 21,963 seconds (6.1 hours)
  • Accelerator utilization: 52.0 percent

With echo factor: \(\EchoFactor\) = 2, inserted after the measured reading, decoding, and augmentation stage.

  • Each already-augmented batch is processed twice, amortizing the measured upstream work; the repeated pass introduces no fresh augmentation
  • Effective throughput: 10,100 images/s, the accelerator’s full rate, because an echo factor above the ratio leaves the accelerator no time waiting
  • Unique images per second: 5,250 images/s (unchanged)
  • Training time: \(90 \times 1.28\text{M}\) / 10,100 images/s = 11,416 seconds (3.2 hours) if echoed data is equally valuable

Trade-off: Repeated samples are not guaranteed to be as useful as fresh samples; the data echoing paper evaluates echo factor, insertion point, and shuffling because those choices determine whether reuse preserves predictive performance. In one network-fed ResNet-50/ImageNet configuration, Choi et al. (2019) reports a 3.25\(\times\) reduction in wall-clock time to the target metric.

Systems insight: Data echoing can trade sample diversity for accelerator utilization when the input pipeline is the bottleneck. For the training chapter’s input-bound ResNet-50, overlap alone left the accelerator about half idle; an echo factor of 2 closes that gap without adding host cores. The useful echo factor must be measured for the workload because it depends on the batch size, insertion point, augmentation, shuffling, and target metric.

Echoing can introduce correlations when the same example appears within or across nearby batches, so implementations must preserve adequate shuffling and validate the target metric. Choi et al. (2019) did not observe a negative batch-normalization interaction in their ResNet-50 or Single Shot MultiBox Detector (SSD) experiments, but that result does not guarantee the same behavior for every workload.

These implementation patterns determine whether data selection yields net systems throughput. Proxy selection curtails probe compute when scoring rankings transfer to target architectures. Sharded file formats and shuffle buffers reconcile pseudo-random batch creation with hardware-level sequential streaming, while data echoing recovers stalled accelerator cycles when ingestion throughput limits the training loop. They still leave a coordination question when several accelerator workers share one host. Each worker scores or trains on its own shard, yet the selected indices and scoring checkpoint must still describe one coherent dataset. Shared storage and host memory do not automatically make each worker’s local view representative of the corpus.

9.7.4 Selection across multiple accelerators

When parallel worker processes divide a dataset into local shards across two to eight accelerators on one host, each worker sees only a fraction of the corpus and may score its shard with a slightly different model checkpoint. Without cross-worker coordination, shard-local coreset rankings miss rare global classes, independent hash tables fail to catch duplicates split across workers, and asynchronous active-learning workers select samples using stale uncertainty scores. A single-process pipeline sorts one global ranking, follows one curriculum schedule, and queries one shared candidate pool. A multi-accelerator pipeline, by contrast, must reconstruct a representative global subset from local shard views while all workers contend for the same host memory capacity, storage bandwidth, and host-to-device links.

Whether distributed selection stays faithful to the global dataset or collapses into local shard heuristics depends on how much cross-worker coordination each technique requires against the bandwidth available to sustain it. On one host, score gathering and index dispatch compete for CPU time and memory bandwidth with decoding and batch preparation, so evaluating selection requires analyzing statistical fidelity alongside these shared resources. Statistical fidelity and coordination cost vary independently across selection algorithms. A coreset can remain representative yet cost too much to coordinate, while a cheap shard-local method can scale but systematically miss rare groups visible only in the global corpus. A distributed design therefore needs two acceptance tests: compare selected-data quality with a centralized or otherwise auditable reference, and compare selection overhead with the end-to-end training time it saves. Passing only one test is insufficient.

Strategies for distributed selection

In a basic data-parallel layout, each worker processes a distinct shard and model synchronization is handled separately. Data selection can add dependencies across those shards (table 9).

The selection dependencies admit two broad architectures, each at a different point in the trade-off between consistency and scalability. Centralized selection keeps selection global while training stays distributed. A coordinator process scores the full dataset and dispatches the selected indices to the workers, which preserves selection quality but makes the coordinator a single bottleneck that can serialize scoring or index dispatch. Whether that overhead is acceptable depends on the selection payload, refresh rate, worker count, and copies through shared host memory, and adding accelerator workers does not increase the host memory available to the coordinator. Hierarchical selection distributes the selection computation itself. Each worker selects candidates from its own shard, and the coordinator merges and reranks them before dispatching the final indices (figure 11). Shard-local selection reduces coordinator load but introduces a quality trade-off, because local quotas and score distributions may not preserve the global ranking or rare groups spread unevenly across shards. The merge step therefore needs normalization, coverage constraints, or a second global pass.

Shard locality affects training as well as selection. Each worker maintains an independent shuffle buffer over a non-overlapping shard. Because randomization is local to each worker’s memory buffer rather than global across the corpus, rare classes or boundary cases concentrated in specific shards receive uneven exposure across workers unless the dataset is stratified and shards are balanced prior to distribution.

Cross-shard deduplication needs the same global view as hierarchical selection. Workers can compute MinHash signatures locally, but a shared merge must compare candidates across shard boundaries. Independent local hash tables cannot detect a duplicate whose counterpart belongs to another worker. The merge must fit the host’s memory budget or operate in bounded batches, rather than assuming that more workers supply more host capacity. Both hierarchical coresets and cross-shard deduplication merge scores or hashes computed against a fixed target; coordination becomes harder when the scoring model itself updates while workers query the pool.

Consistency challenges in active learning

Shard-local selection already needs a coherent score scale, but active learning introduces an additional complication because the model changes during selection. Consider what happens when Worker A scores samples using the model at step \(\OptimizerStepIndex\) while Worker B simultaneously updates the model to step \(\OptimizerStepIndex+1\). Worker A’s scores are now stale and may select samples that the updated model would rank differently.

The scoring schedule therefore defines the meaning of a query, not merely its cost. A reproducible system versions the model checkpoint, candidate-pool snapshot, scoring rule, and selected indices together. It can then measure how much ranking agreement decays with checkpoint age and choose a refresh interval from that evidence rather than from an arbitrary number of steps.

Several strategies mitigate this staleness problem, each with distinct overhead characteristics:

  • Synchronous scoring: All workers pause training and score simultaneously, guaranteeing consistency but at substantial cost in accelerator utilization.
  • Periodic score refresh: Workers re-score every \(\ScoreRefreshInterval\) epochs rather than every batch, trading freshness for reduced overhead.
  • Checkpoint-robust selection: The system selects samples that show high uncertainty under multiple model checkpoints, favoring decisions that are less sensitive to checkpoint choice. Agreement across past checkpoints is a robustness heuristic; ranking quality still needs validation as the model evolves.

Positive ROI can erode quickly when workers coordinate frequently during training. Selection across workers adds coordination overhead whose size depends on what is synchronized and how often. That overhead must remain smaller than the training time saved, and when it approaches that limit, a simpler strategy or a longer refresh interval restores the margin.

The communication path adds a further constraint. Gradient synchronization may use accelerator collectives over local interconnects, while embedding vectors, score arrays, and coreset indices may pass through host memory and CPU dispatch. Selection can therefore expose host-memory, PCIe, storage, or process-synchronization bottlenecks that the training profile alone does not reveal. Extending the design to independent machines adds network topology and remote-storage constraints; those are a further scaling problem beyond the one-host design developed here.

Operational feasibility, however, does not guarantee economic viability. A deduplication pipeline that costs more to build than one training run saves loses money on a single run and pays off only if enough runs reuse it. Deploying selection techniques therefore requires financial accounting of capital, compute, and human annotation budgets.

Self-Check: Question

  1. Why do naive random sample lookups across non-contiguous indices in large un-sharded dataset files severely degrade I/O throughput on storage hardware, and how do shuffle buffers mitigate this?

    1. Random lookups bypass host CPU caches, forcing floating-point registers to re-encode all labels
    2. Random lookups violate PCIe parity checks, causing GPU kernel timeouts during backward passes
    3. Random lookups trigger hash collisions in the Python garbage collector, halting dataloading threads
    4. Random 4 KB reads fall below peak sequential storage bandwidth due to IOPS limits, by hundreds of times on HDDs, whereas shuffle buffers read large sequential chunks and randomize locally in memory

  2. Contrast upstream data echoing (echoing before data augmentation) with downstream data echoing (echoing after augmentation) in terms of computational overhead and sample diversity.

  3. True or False: If the GPU training step takes 20 ms and the CPU data loading/augmentation pipeline takes 10 ms (\(R = T_{\text{pipeline}} / T_{\text{GPU}} = 0.5\)), applying a data echoing factor of \(e = 2\) will double the end-to-end training throughput.

  4. On one host with two to eight accelerator workers, how can centralized coreset selection become a bottleneck, and what trade-off does hierarchical selection introduce?

    1. Centralized selection requires all workers to share a single GPU; hierarchical selection distributes weights across SSDs
    2. Centralized selection fails because sharding prevents network cards from transmitting floating-point values
    3. Centralized selection can bottleneck host memory and coordinator dispatch; hierarchical selection prunes locally per shard, risking loss of globally rare samples across shards
    4. Centralized selection eliminates gradient synchronization; hierarchical selection disables local backpropagation

  5. In distributed active learning, Worker A scores candidate samples using checkpoint step \(t\), while Worker B has already advanced the shared model to step \(t+100\). What consistency challenge arises, and what is the best response?

    1. Worker A encounters deadlock in CUDA streams; disable autograd during scoring
    2. Worker B overwrites Worker A’s local storage; mount the dataset read-only
    3. Worker A’s GPU runs out of memory; reduce batch size to 1
    4. Worker A may produce stale rankings that differ from fresher ones; version checkpoints and choose the score-refresh interval from measured ranking agreement

  6. Explain why performing independent, shard-local coreset pruning on an unstratified, partitioned dataset can cause minority class collapse during distributed training.
