9.8 Choosing a Selection Strategy

The hospital team in notebook 9.3 could not label every scan, because doing so would have cost $5,000,000, 10× over its budget. Allocating capital between human annotation and accelerator compute is a primary systems decision, and a team under a fixed budget must determine whether the next dollar buys more downstream accuracy as specialist annotations or as additional accelerator hours. A cost model settles the question. The choice among levers follows from it in four steps: the total cost of data, the break-even point of a selection pipeline, the amortization of one-time investments across the runs that share them, and a decision process worked on the ResNet-50 vision team of section 9.2.6.

9.8.1 Data costs and return on investment

Budgeting a training project from cloud GPU and object-storage invoices alone misses the licensing and human annotation bills that often dwarf compute cost. The total cost of training data spans acquisition, labeling, storage retention, and accelerator compute across the full data lifecycle: \[ \TotalDataCost= \DataAcquisitionCost+ \DataLabelingCost+ \DataStorageCost+ \DataProcessingCost\] Labeling (\(\DataLabelingCost\)) represents the expenditure that data selection targets most directly, whereas storage (\(\DataStorageCost\)) and processing (\(\DataProcessingCost\)) scale mechanically with retained byte volume and the number of accelerator passes. Table 10 itemizes these four cost components alongside their operational drivers.

The interplay of these four cost terms becomes concrete when evaluating an ImageNet-scale vision model training run. Calculating each term explicitly shows how easily data acquisition and human labeling outspend the accelerator hours needed to fit the weights.

Napkin Math 9.6: Cost breakdown: ImageNet-scale training
Table 11 itemizes one illustrative acquisition, labeling, storage, and compute scenario at ImageNet scale. The resulting ratio follows from the stated licensing, annotation, storage, runtime, and compute-cost assumptions; it is not a typical ratio for every supervised workload.

Systems insight: Acquisition and labeling can dwarf compute cost in supervised vision workloads, as they do under these assumptions. The compute figure covers the whole training campaign at the compute-bound rate of notebook 9.4, not one run: a published model is the survivor of sweeps, restarts, and ablations, and costing a single run would understate compute roughly thirtyfold. Even so, acquisition and labeling remain the larger share. The run count is an assumption like any other, so a complete ROI calculation must state it and count both categories rather than assuming which one dominates.

Data selection techniques insert algorithmic filtering into the training pipeline to reduce downstream costs. Scoring consumes accelerator cycles, generates host-device memory traffic, or requires custom pipeline stages, so a strategy is justified only if the downstream savings exceed these overheads. Return on investment (ROI), the savings net of the investment per unit invested, makes that test explicit. Because a selection investment is often reused across a training campaign, the general form counts the \(\TrainingRunCount\) runs that share it, the amortized ROI: \[ \text{Amortized ROI} = \frac{\TrainingRunCount\times \text{Per-Run Savings} - \text{One-Time Investment}}{\text{One-Time Investment}} \times 100\% \tag{3}\]

A single run sets \(\TrainingRunCount= 1\) and recovers the ordinary ratio of savings minus investment to investment, whereas the balance between investment and savings varies across selection strategies. Table 12 maps the primary cost drivers and benefit mechanisms across four standard techniques; their net return depends on corpus volume, model scale, and existing pipeline infrastructure.

The profiles in table 12 compare those costs directly. Deduplication requires an upfront investment in hashing or embedding infrastructure, but can pay off across many epochs and runs by eliminating redundant storage and compute. Coreset selection demands training a proxy model to score the dataset; it only breaks even if the compute saved by dropping samples exceeds the cost of that proxy training. Active learning shifts the cost from human labelers to machine inference, generating high ROI when human time is expensive, though it introduces human-in-the-loop latency that can stall automated pipelines. Finally, data augmentation requires zero new data acquisition but shifts the burden to the CPU or GPU, trading compute cycles for effective dataset expansion. Each technique is worth adopting only when its savings exceed what it costs to set up and run, which makes locating that exact crossover threshold the next step.

9.8.2 Break-even analysis

If candidate scoring is computationally heavy or the unlabeled pool is massive, a selection policy can spend more money scoring candidates than it saves on omitted labels or training steps. The break-even point marks the operating boundary where the financial savings from omitted samples equal the computational and operational overhead of selecting them. Suppose labeling costs $10/sample, active learning starts from 1,000 labeled samples ($10,000), and each round issues 100 queries at $50 inference cost (the expense of scoring the candidate pool \(\UnlabeledPool\) with the active model). If the random-labeling baseline requires 5,000 samples for target accuracy while active learning reaches the same target accuracy with 2,000 labeled samples, the labels-plus-scoring comparison reports net savings per dollar of the stated active-learning budget (using the active-learning budget in the denominator rather than the one-time investment of equation 3): \[\begin{gather*} \text{Random labeling cost} = \text{5,000} \times \text{\$10/sample} = \text{\$50,000} \\ \text{Active learning cost} = \text{2,000} \times \text{\$10/sample} + \text{10 rounds} \times \text{\$50} = \text{\$20,500} \\ \text{ROI} = \frac{\text{\$50,000} - \text{\$20,500}}{\text{\$20,500}}= \text{$143.9\%$} \end{gather*}\]

Within this labels-plus-scoring boundary, break-even occurs when avoided labeling costs equal selection overhead. A complete dollar comparison must also deduct any additional retraining, engineering, storage, and delay-related expense relative to the random-labeling baseline. When an unlabeled candidate pool \(\UnlabeledPool\) contains millions of uncurated examples, repeatedly scoring it across multiple rounds scales scoring cost with \(|\UnlabeledPool| \times \CandidateScoringCost\). An algorithmic reduction in labeled samples can then be erased if proxy inference costs are not carefully bounded. When one fixed budget funds both scoring and labeling, every dollar spent scoring the pool is a dollar withdrawn from labels, so practical loops narrow \(\UnlabeledPool\) with metadata or a cheap heuristic before the active model scores it. Even a pipeline that loses money on a single run, however, can cross break-even once its output is reused across an entire training campaign.

9.8.3 Amortization across training runs

Charging the entire engineering and preprocessing bill of a static curation pipeline to a single training run makes a profitable campaign investment look like a net loss. Filters such as MinHash deduplication, exact \(\NgramLength\)-gram indexing, and embedding clustering require substantial upfront engineering and compute to build once. Production model development, by contrast, is inherently iterative: hyperparameter search, architecture sweeps, ablation studies, and seed checks repeatedly train on the same curated corpus. The amortized ROI of equation 3 therefore spreads that upfront curation investment across every run in the campaign, as table 13 itemizes for an illustrative MinHash deduplication pipeline.

Because the initial infrastructure and signature-generation costs are fixed, each subsequent training run compounds the net financial return. Three operational factors govern whether curation infrastructure reaches break-even:

  • Repeated training runs: Hyperparameter search, model iterations, and scheduled retraining reuse the same selection infrastructure many times.
  • Shared datasets: A cleaned or deduplicated corpus can support multiple teams or model architectures.
  • Broadly reusable techniques: Methods such as deduplication transfer across models, whereas task-specific coresets may not.

Table 14 tracks how amortized return scales with campaign run count under these cost parameters. A pipeline that loses money on a one-off experiment crosses break-even within a handful of retraining cycles and compounds its savings across larger campaigns.

Deduplication achieves high investment leverage because eliminating redundant sequences reduces host-storage footprint and avoids wasted gradient updates across every downstream architecture. Sample-selection methods conditioned on proxy gradients or uncertainty scores transfer within a model family or between related architectures, but their rankings must be revalidated when the target model, task, or data changes, which narrows their amortization window relative to deduplication. For exploratory or one-off training runs, low-overhead methods like uniform random sampling or basic augmentation may offer better ROI. A static scoring pass can still pay for itself in one run if its measured savings exceed its cost at target quality.

Checkpoint 9.3: Accepting a data intervention

A smaller training pool is one observation in the acceptance decision. Connect the measurements to the constraint the intervention was meant to relieve.

With time, labeling, and amortized costs expressed in one accounting, every technique in the chapter can now be weighed against the claim it makes. A training-time saving must satisfy the selection inequality, a labeling saving must beat the random-labeling baseline after scoring and any other differing costs, and a reusable investment must recover its one-time cost across the runs or tasks that share it. Costing each technique separately, however, does not say which one to reach for first when several of them compete for the same budget.

9.8.4 Decision process

When labeling budget, redundancy, rare classes, privacy, and convergence speed conflict, systems engineering requires identifying the primary binding constraint. Table 15 collects the three levers with order-of-magnitude ranges drawn from scenarios like those worked in this chapter; each must reduce the cost it targets after counting its overhead and preserving target quality and required coverage. Self-supervised pretraining, which changes the training signal rather than the sample set, enters the decision through the labeling-cost branch of the tree that follows.

Once the dominant constraint is named, it points to a candidate technique, and the five lighthouse workloads, each bound by a different resource, show the pairing in practice. Privacy carries a condition of its own. When privacy requirements limit direct use of real records, privacy-audited synthesis can reduce that use, provided the synthetic data is checked for leakage of the records it was derived from.

Lighthouse 9.1: Lighthouse data selection
Data selection principles apply to all five lighthouse models, though the dominant constraint determines the technique. Table 16 maps each workload to a priority. Compute-bound ResNet-50 training benefits from coreset selection, while GPT-2 XL training relies on deduplication and quality filtering to cut the raw tokens it must process. MobileNetV2 is compute-bound in its efficient regime, and its binding deployment constraints, latency and power, apply at inference, where no choice of training data changes the cost of a prediction. The data lever that matters is therefore the one that helps a small model generalize, augmentation matched to its capacity (compact networks underfit under distortion that a large model absorbs). The work is validating that the augmentation preserves the invariances the deployment environment requires. DLRM, the capacity-bound lighthouse, spends much of its terabyte-scale embedding capacity on cold IDs that appear rarely in training data. It uses interaction deduplication to cut redundant examples and embedding pruning to shrink those tables, but removing repeated interactions shrinks a table only when IDs disappear or the allocation, hashing, or sharing policy changes. Finally, keyword spotting, the power-bound lighthouse, is bound for data selection by a scarce, costly seed set as much as by its power and memory budget. It overcomes that scarcity by using augmentation and synthesis to build robust datasets from minimal seed samples.

Across these workloads, data selection functions as a systems optimization tailored to relieve whichever physical resource binds the pipeline.

The lighthouse mapping assumes a single dominant constraint, but real projects face several at once. The triage flowchart in figure 12 therefore routes candidate strategies hierarchically from the root bottleneck (labeling expense, compute limits, or data scarcity). Operational checks on oracle availability, dataset redundancy, teacher or simulator support, and unlabeled-pool size then isolate actionable techniques.

The tree identifies candidates; an engineering decision must still test their cost and quality. The vision workload in example 9.2 provides a concrete comparison. Its target-model and proxy scorers retain the same fraction of data, but they need not retain the same examples. A cheaper ranking can therefore win the time comparison and lose the deployment-quality comparison.

Step 1: Assess the bottleneck

The vision team’s stated objective is shorter training turnaround at the existing quality target. The full-data baseline takes 211 minutes, and both candidates retain 10 percent of the images. This places the decision in the compute-cost branch. The retained fraction alone does not settle it; the selection inequality and the slice-coverage check do.

A different binding constraint would change the comparison. If labels were the limiting expense, fewer annotations could justify additional training time. If controlled sweeps instead diagnosed a shortage of useful tokens, the data-scarcity branch would be relevant even with idle accelerators. Naming the objective determines which saving the candidate must actually deliver.

Step 2: Check prerequisites

Both vision candidates require access to the full image pool, a scoring model, and stable sample indices. The proxy additionally needs a ranking that transfers to the target model; its lower execution cost does not establish that prerequisite. Table 17 places these requirements alongside those of the alternative branches.

Step 3: Estimate ROI

For the stated vision scenario, target-model scoring plus subset training takes 21.8 minutes (Option A), while proxy scoring plus subset training takes 21.4 minutes (Option B). These figures evaluate the marginal scoring pass using existing checkpoints. Both options beat the full-data baseline in the cost model. The proxy reduces only the scoring term; the retained training work is identical.

Suppose pilot validation in this hypothetical decision confirms that both runs reach the aggregate target under the modeled schedule, but only Option A meets every deployment-critical slice threshold. Option B regresses on a rare class. Option A is then the acceptable choice despite its more expensive scoring pass, saving 188.8 minutes against the baseline. Option B remains a candidate for repair, not deployment: enforce the coverage floor from section 9.3.4, rescore or enlarge the subset as needed, and repeat the time-to-quality comparison. Additional examples or passes change its cost, so the original proxy saving cannot simply be carried forward. If both candidates passed all quality gates at the stated times, Option B would win this time comparison.

A time saving must still justify any engineering investment. If the proxy checkpoint does not already exist and its training is charged entirely to a single run, that preparatory training erases Option B’s modest time advantage over Option A. Section 9.8.1 accounts for that investment, and section 9.8.3 spreads one-time costs across the \(\TrainingRunCount\) planned experiments. Reusing the proxy is economically useful only while its ranking continues to pass the same quality checks.

Step 4: Combine techniques

Data selection techniques are not mutually exclusive, but combining them does not guarantee additive gains. A representative workflow deduplicates the raw corpus, applies a coverage-aware coreset filter, orders retained batches through an empirical curriculum, and applies runtime augmentations during batch collation. Pretrained representations alter the baseline trajectory further. Every additional stage must justify its compute overhead, because cascading filters frequently discard overlapping subsets while their scoring latencies accumulate. Stages compound efficiency gains only when their effects do not overlap and their overhead remains small, so combined savings must be measured rather than assumed.

A choice made at the data layer does not stay there. This decision process weighs a technique by its own scoring cost and the training work it removes, but a smaller or reordered corpus also changes what the model learns to represent, and the compression and deployment stages downstream inherit that representation. A selection that looks optimal in isolation can therefore move cost into those later stages, so the chosen strategy must be checked against the layers it feeds.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A company invests \(\$30{,}000\) to compute a high-quality coreset. Training on the full dataset costs \(\$10{,}000\) per run, whereas training on the coreset costs \(\$4{,}000\) per run. What is the break-even number of training runs, and what is the ROI after 10 runs?

    1. Break-even is 5 runs, and ROI after 10 runs is \(100\%\)
    2. Break-even is 3 runs, and ROI after 10 runs is \(300\%\)
    3. Break-even is 8 runs, and ROI after 10 runs is \(50\%\)
    4. Break-even is 10 runs, and ROI after 10 runs is \(0\%\)

  2. Using the chapter’s cost model \(C_{\text{total}} = C_{\text{acquire}} + C_{\text{label}} + C_{\text{store}} + C_{\text{process}}\), which scenario best justifies an upstream filtering investment?

    1. Skip filtering so that maximum raw volume reaches training, regardless of downstream processing and labeling cost
    2. Spend \(\$5{,}000\) on automated filtering only if measured avoided labeling, storage, and processing costs exceed that amount while task coverage and target quality still hold
    3. Double human labeling effort to manually review every raw sample before filtering, because filtering cost is always worse than labeling cost
    4. Cut evaluation entirely to offset the cost of training on unfiltered data

  3. Explain why calculating Return on Investment (ROI) for data selection requires tracking engineering implementation and pipeline maintenance costs in addition to raw accelerator compute hours.

  4. According to the chapter’s decision framework, if an ML team has an abundant pool of unlabeled domain data, very limited annotation budget, and access to human domain experts for selective queries, which technique branch is recommended?

    1. Pure transformation-based data augmentation without labeling
    2. Active learning (human-in-the-loop selective query) or semi-supervised learning
    3. Generative self-instruct synthesis to replace all human annotators entirely
    4. Exhaustive pairwise Jaccard deduplication across all unlabeled samples

  5. When deciding between static coreset pruning and dynamic online active selection for a production pipeline, what role does the expected number of training runs (\(N\)) play in the architectural choice?

9.9 Cross-Layer Interactions

Halving a training corpus halves the forward and backward passes, yet the resulting model may be no cheaper to compress or serve. Real ML systems combine data selection with model compression and hardware acceleration, and these cross-layer interactions amplify or undermine each other in ways no single layer reveals. Model compression (post-training weight pruning, lower-precision representation, and distillation) acts on the trained network, and its arithmetic reductions reach serving latency only when the target execution substrate and runtime kernels support the resulting sparsity or precision patterns (Hoefler et al. 2021). Because the training corpus shapes the representations that compression later works on, a model trained on an uncurated, repetitive corpus can spend capacity on redundant features that post-training pruning then discards, whereas a curated corpus of diverse, informative examples can yield more compact representations from the start. That effect is an empirical hypothesis to verify, not a property of every selected dataset. The two levers therefore compose without substituting for each other: data selection reduces training work and shapes learned representations, model compression shrinks the deployment artifact, and their combined impact is measured on serving latency and task accuracy rather than inferred from the upstream dataset reduction.

Hoefler, Torsten, Dan Alistarh, Tal Ben-Nun, Nikoli Dryden, and Alexandra Peste. 2021. “Sparsity in Deep Learning: Pruning and Growth for Efficient Inference and Training in Neural Networks.” Journal of Machine Learning Research 22 (241): 1–124.

↪ Why accelerators turn structured sparsity patterns into wall-clock speedup while unstructured sparsity rarely pays off is developed in N:M structured sparsity mechanics.

↪ Co-designing post-training pruning and quantization under the silicon contract is developed in Fitting the Deployment Budget.

Production deployments execute these techniques as one pipeline in two stages: data selection and training turn raw inputs into a trained model, and post-training compression and hardware acceleration then deploy that artifact (figure 13). Every stage along this end-to-end path amplifies or attenuates downstream efficiency.

Tracing artifacts through the pipeline in figure 13 shows why data selection sits at the head of the optimization stack. Pruning raw inputs reduces upstream training FLOPs, but it does not automatically shrink parameter footprint, improve quantization headroom, or relax serving latency constraints in the downstream compression and hardware stages. Each downstream tier inherits both the computational savings and any representational deficits created upstream. An aggressive filter that discards rare but critical edge cases can force downstream engineers to train longer or abandon aggressive quantization to preserve task accuracy, turning an apparent upstream saving into a production failure when selection assumptions go unchecked.

Self-Check: Question

  1. In an idealized compute-bound training scenario with fixed target quality and negligible new overheads, a team halves the number of processed examples, halves the operations per processed example on the same compute-critical path, and doubles realized arithmetic throughput. What total speedup follows from the chapter’s multiplicative D·A·M reasoning?

    1. An \(8\times\) speedup, because halving workload, halving per-sample work, and doubling throughput multiply on the same critical path
    2. A \(6\times\) speedup, because the factors add across layers
    3. A \(2\times\) speedup, because only the hardware improvement matters once arithmetic throughput doubles
    4. A \(4\times\) speedup, because reducing training examples and reducing per-sample work are the same effect

  2. Why are upstream data selection and downstream model compression complementary rather than interchangeable?

    1. Model compression can only be applied to computer vision models, whereas data selection is restricted to NLP
    2. Data selection eliminates backward passes entirely, whereas model compression eliminates forward passes
    3. Data selection optimizes only inference latency on edge devices, whereas model compression only affects training time
    4. Data selection reduces how many examples are processed, whereas model compression reduces the artifact size and can lower execution cost per example on compatible hardware

  3. A team curates its training corpus to half its original size and reports that the resulting model will be cheaper to deploy. What does the reduction actually change, and what evidence would support the deployment claim?

  4. Explain how an aggressive data filter that discards rare but critical edge cases can raise costs in the compression and deployment stages of the optimization stack.

9.10 Fallacies and Pitfalls

Scaling a training corpus tenfold can multiply storage, I/O, and accelerator bills while barely moving test accuracy. Yet an uncalibrated selection filter can just as easily spend more time scoring candidates than it saves in training or silently erase a safety-critical rare class. These failures fall into three groups: conceptual fallacies about what data selection can achieve, implementation pitfalls that arise when correct strategies meet systems bottlenecks, and transfer errors that occur when benchmark results are applied uncritically to new domains.

Fallacy: More data always improves the model enough to justify its cost.

More data does not imply proportional accuracy gains because learning curves follow power-law diminishing returns. Test loss diminishes sub-linearly with dataset size (\(\LossFunction(\DatasetSize) \propto \DataScalingLossFactor\) for a fitted exponent \(\DataScalingExponent\)), meaning each successive error reduction demands a multiplicatively larger corpus. In an illustrative learning-curve scenario, scaling a dataset tenfold from 1M to 10M samples yields only 4 percentage points of accuracy gain. That 10\(\times\) volume expansion still costs a tenfold increase in storage I/O, host-to-device transfers, and accelerator FLOPs. Teams should measure the learning curve of their own workload rather than assume that more data delivers proportional value.

Pitfall: Replacing real-data validation with synthetic-only training data.

Synthetic samples cost almost nothing to acquire, so generative models can look like a replacement for data collection. Synthetic-only training fails in two ways. First, synthetic data can differ from the deployment distribution (section 9.5.3, figure 8), so the model learns features that fail to transfer; only real-data labels reveal the effect. Second, recursive training on model-generated data can trigger model collapse (Shumailov et al. 2024), which progressively erodes distribution tails: in this scenario, evaluation accuracy degrades from 95 percent to 78 percent across five recursive generations, suffering a 17 percentage-point collapse. Synthetic mixtures must therefore be validated against curated real data.

Illustrative model collapse: accuracy erodes with each recursive synthetic generation.

Fallacy: Data selection is merely data cleaning.

A dataset can be completely clean and correctly labeled yet redundant: once the model has minimized loss on a feature cluster, further identical samples add almost no gradient signal and still consume memory bandwidth and accelerator FLOPs. Cleaning corrects intrinsic defects; selection raises the learning return per FLOP by choosing examples for task-relevant information and coverage (figure 5; section 9.3.4). In the worked coreset scenario of notebook 9.1, the selected subset reached 1.8× higher ICR.

Pitfall: Treating data selection as a budget-only tactic.

Selection can look like a technique for resource-constrained teams, as if frontier foundation models could brute-force convergence by scaling the cluster. The data wall binds at every scale. At frontier scale, token consumption converges toward the finite ceiling of high-quality human text (Training Data Approaching the Stock of Public Text); at GPT-2 scale, one eight-H100 machine passes over the whole WebText corpus about 17 times in 4.4 days (section 9.1). The economic return of selection also scales with cluster expenditure: a 10 percent efficiency gain on a $100M frontier training run saves $10M under these assumptions. Amortizing scores and cached embeddings across repeated runs (section 9.8.3) strengthens the case further.

Fallacy: Selection overhead is too small to change training economics.

The selection inequality (equation 2) requires \(\SelectionTime+ \TrainingTime(\SubsetDatasetSize) < \TrainingTime(\FullDatasetSize)\) at matched quality. Consider a baseline training run requiring 8 hours, where training on an optimized coreset takes 2 hours. If a complex selection algorithm requires 10 hours to compute sample importance, the selection step alone consumes 5× the duration of the accelerated training run and exceeds the full training baseline itself, a net wall-clock loss. In the illustrative comparison, proxy-based EL2N scoring completes in 30 minutes, which is 6.2 percent of the full-training baseline and satisfies the inequality.

Pitfall: Pruning rare classes without a coverage floor.

Standard selection objectives minimize aggregate loss, which gives tail slices little influence over sampling. In a 1M dataset where rare classes comprise 0.1 percent of the data (1,000 examples), an unstratified 10 percent coreset retains only 100 rare-class examples in expectation, far below the illustrative 150-sample retention floor assumed here. An appropriate floor must be validated from task-specific learning curves and critical-slice performance. Importance sampling can compensate by weighting losses by inverse selection probabilities, but extreme weights inflate gradient variance and force smaller learning rates. As recommended in section 9.3.4, production coreset pipelines enforce hard stratification floors per class or critical slice before spending the remaining selection budget.

Fallacy: Deduplicating training data is enough to make evaluation reliable.

Web-scale pipelines scrape training, validation, and benchmark splits from common crawls, which introduces silent cross-split leakage. When an evaluation set contains near-duplicates of training documents, the metric measures memorization rather than generalization. A deduplication study found that train-test overlap affects over 4 percent of validation examples across standard language-modeling benchmarks (Lee et al. 2022). Deduplicating the training partition alone leaves this contamination untouched; cross-partition deduplication is required, using \(\NgramLength\)-gram matching or MinHash LSH (section 9.3.2). For a frozen evaluation set, the fix removes the overlapping training records and preserves the evaluation set and its provenance.

Lee, Katherine, Daphne Ippolito, Andrew Nystrom, Chiyuan Zhang, Douglas Eck, Chris Callison-Burch, and Nicholas Carlini. 2022. “Deduplicating Training Data Makes Language Models Better.” Proceedings of the 60th Annual Meeting of the Association for Computational Linguistics (Volume 1: Long Papers), 8424–45. https://doi.org/10.18653/v1/2022.acl-long.577.

Pitfall: Active learning without considering annotation latency.

Textbook formulations of active learning assume an oracle that labels queries instantly. Production annotation queues introduce delays such as the illustrative 14-day latency between sample dispatch and label return assumed here. This latency breaks the active learning feedback loop in two ways. If accelerator clusters halt training while awaiting labels, expensive hardware sits idle, destroying cluster compute utilization. Conversely, if training continues asynchronously on background data while annotations are pending, model weights drift across training steps; by the time the labels arrive, the uncertainty scores that justified querying those samples are obsolete. Dispatching large batches to amortize the turnaround adds redundancy within the batch unless diversity penalties are applied.

Fallacy: If a technique works on ImageNet, it will work on my dataset.

CIFAR-10 and ImageNet are highly redundant across canonical object classes, which allows coreset pruning of often 30 to 50 percent without measurable accuracy loss. Histopathology, satellite imagery, and industrial defect detection have heavy-tailed feature distributions in which safety-critical patterns appear in a few rare samples. A 50 percent cut tuned on ImageNet can remove the few examples that separate subtle pathologies. Geometric coreset algorithms also depend on embeddings, and a feature extractor pretrained on natural images may not resolve fine-grained domain distinctions. Teams should measure the redundancy of their own data and validate retention across critical slices before pruning.

Pitfall: Optimizing data selection metrics instead of deployment metrics.

Selection algorithms optimize aggregate surrogates such as area under the learning curve (AULC) or performance-per-data (PPD, section 9.2.4). A selection policy retaining a 10 percent coreset can achieve superior aggregate PPD by prioritizing dense clusters of common, easy samples that accelerate average convergence, while discarding ambiguous edge cases and rare slices. Production failures, however, occur on tail slices, edge cases, and protected sub-populations rather than on averages. A policy without subgroup constraints can pass global validation and still fail in deployment. As the measurement framework of section 9.2 requires, policies must be evaluated on stratified, slice-level benchmarks, even when doing so slightly lowers average PPD.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A research paper reports that an EL2N coreset selection method successfully pruned \(50\%\) of CIFAR-10 with \(0\%\) loss in accuracy. A medical imaging team applies the exact same \(50\%\) pruning ratio to a rare tumor detection dataset and suffers a disastrous \(28\%\) drop in recall. What fallacy explains this failure?

    1. The team failed to use GPU acceleration during the inference pass
    2. The team used float32 precision instead of bfloat16 mixed precision
    3. CIFAR-10 contains more total classes than medical imaging datasets
    4. Assuming that benchmark coreset pruning ratios transfer directly to specialized, highly imbalanced production domains with rare failure modes

  2. A team implements an elaborate multi-stage active learning pipeline that reduces training dataset size by \(40\%\). However, the continuous clustering, proxy scoring, and cross-worker all-gather synchronization take 3 times longer than the GPU time saved during training. Which pitfall does this represent?

    1. Violating the Selection Inequality by incurring selection overheads that exceed downstream training savings (\(T_{\text{selection}} > \Delta T_{\text{train}}\))
    2. Encountering model collapse due to recursive generator loops
    3. Failing to implement 4 KB small-read alignment on host NVMe storage
    4. Violating the smoothness assumption in semi-supervised consistency regularization

  3. Why is evaluating a data selection strategy solely on aggregate validation accuracy a dangerous pitfall when dealing with imbalanced datasets?

  4. True or False: Because modern high-capacity generative models produce photorealistic images and fluent text, a model trained on recursively generated synthetic data will never suffer performance degradation.

9.11 Summary

Feeding every available example into a training run wastes accelerator compute, storage bandwidth, and annotation budget on redundant or noisy records because marginal learning signal varies by orders of magnitude across raw samples. Data selection therefore treats training volume as an engineering variable rather than a fixed constraint, minimizing total cost across compute, storage, labeling, energy, and wall-clock time so a curated subset can match or outperform a much larger uncurated corpus.

This systems perspective becomes actionable through three quantitative tools: the ICR, the selection inequality, and the total data cost model. The information-compute ratio quantifies learning gained per floating-point operation, providing a proxy for sample value that must be evaluated alongside coverage and target quality. Controlled model-size and data sweeps diagnose whether a run is data-limited or compute-limited in the first place, and PPD, AULC, and DCR then show whether a reduction delivered what it promised across the learning curve. The selection inequality tests a training-time claim by requiring scoring plus subset training to take less time than full-data training at matched quality, while return-on-investment and break-even analysis determine whether a proposed selection mechanism saves resources or merely shifts expenditure between stages.

The three levers act at different points: static pruning removes redundancy before training, dynamic selection prioritizes informative examples during training, and synthetic generation creates data where none exists. Together, these levers answer the data wall, the gap between rapidly growing compute capacity and slowly growing high-quality data.

Learning from unlabeled data sits beside these levers rather than within them. Semi-supervised learning stretches a small labeled seed across an unlabeled pool, while self-supervised pretraining moves the bulk of representation learning into an unannotated upstream phase so downstream tasks can reach target quality with far fewer task-specific labels. Under the worked amortization scenario’s assumptions, fine-tuning uses 100× fewer labels per task and reaches the compute-only crossover at roughly 10.5 tasks, while a full dollar comparison also weighs labeling savings against compute prices and other differing costs.

In production, the difficult part is rarely the scoring rule itself; it is keeping selection from becoming the next bottleneck. Proxy models and cached embeddings bound scoring time, shard-based loaders keep storage access sequential, and data echoing recovers accelerator cycles when ingestion lags. On one H100, a half-size ResNet-50 coreset saves about 63 seconds and 44 kJ per epoch against a one-time scoring pass of about 42 seconds, and those seconds are realized only when the host keeps the accelerator fed and cross-worker coordination stays off the critical path.

Every row of the opening chapter map (table 1) has now been derived, and the formulas behind its tests fit on one page (table 18). Together they let an engineer estimate, before committing a training run, whether a lever saves more than it costs.

Key Takeaways: Curate, do not accumulate
  • Selection optimizes system cost: The objective is minimizing total cost across compute, storage, labeling, and energy rather than reducing sample counts alone. The information-compute ratio measures learning gained per FLOP spent; total-cost accounting and pipeline measurements establish whether that gain serves the system objective.
  • Start with deduplication: Exact deduplication is often the lowest-risk first technique because it removes repeated bytes without a learned scoring pass. Apply near-duplicate and semantic deduplication only after validating thresholds against downstream quality metrics.
  • Training-time savings must exceed selection overhead: At matched quality, \(\SelectionTime+ \TrainingTime(\SubsetDatasetSize) < \TrainingTime(\FullDatasetSize)\). Lightweight proxy models and cached embeddings keep \(\SelectionTime\) low, whereas unoptimized selection algorithms can consume all the compute they save.
  • Dynamic selection adapts the training mix: Curriculum learning changes presentation order, while active learning changes which samples receive labels. Either improves efficiency when its scoring rule, coverage, and refresh overhead are validated.
  • Self-supervised pretraining can amortize adaptation cost: Pretraining once and fine-tuning many times spreads pretraining cost across downstream tasks. The worked scenario assumes a 100× label reduction and a 20× marginal-compute reduction; realized savings depend on transfer quality and reuse.
  • Synthetic data is a supplement, not a replacement: Synthetic samples augment scarce tail cases effectively, but cannot substitute entirely for empirical data. Unanchored synthetic training introduces domain-gap degradation, and recursive synthetic loops trigger catastrophic model collapse.
  • Validated workload reduction compounds downstream: When selection safely eliminates examples or tokens without adding compensating steps, that work never reaches later model or machine optimizations. Dynamic scoring and synthesis add their own costs, so the combined saving must be measured rather than assumed.

Examples differ greatly in how much they teach per byte transferred and per FLOP executed, and selection spends compute on the ones that teach most. Within the D·A·M taxonomy, selection operates at the head of the system: every redundant sample or uninformative token safely eliminated directly reduces operation count (\(\Operations\)) in the iron law of ML systems (principle 3) and can avoid downstream training reads. Scoring may still read the entire pool, and shard streaming may read discarded records, so reductions in data volume (\(\DataVolume\)) must count scoring and shard-read traffic separately. When selection overhead satisfies the inequality at matched quality, the reduction removes work before downstream compression or hardware acceleration sees it. Model-based scoring offers a way to invest additional compute in selection, consistent with the bitter lesson, but a stronger scorer justifies that investment only if its improved rankings save more downstream work than it costs. The Pareto frontier (principle 5) applies upstream of the model as well. Each retained fraction is an operating point that trades coverage and rare-slice accuracy against compute, and no single fraction is optimal for every workload.

What’s Next: From data to algorithms
Data selection changes what the system learns from. Model Compression turns to how the model represents what it has learned. It starts from the deployment envelope the model must fit and adjusts three things to fit it: the model’s structure through pruning and distillation, its numerical precision through quantization, and the way the remaining operations map onto the hardware that executes them.

9.12 Self-Check Answers

Self-Check: Answer

  1. In an ML infrastructure scaling scenario where available GPU compute grows by approximately \(10\times\) every 3 years while high-quality web data grows by only \(2\times\) every 5 years, what primary systems regime emerges, and what is the appropriate systems response?

    1. A compute-rich, data-constrained data wall regime where intelligent data selection and curation must maximize the learning signal extracted per token
    2. A memory bandwidth-bound regime where model parallel sharding must replace data parallelism across all training clusters
    3. An I/O ingestion bottleneck where disk read bandwidth must be quadrupled to keep GPUs saturated
    4. A compute-starved regime where synthetic data generation should be eliminated to avoid wasting accelerator cycles

    Answer: The correct answer is A. Compute throughput growing faster than accessible high-quality human data creates a compute-to-data imbalance known as the data wall. In this regime, additional raw compute yields diminishing returns on uncurated corpora, making data selection essential to maximize the Information-Compute Ratio (ICR). Shifting model parallelism addresses parameter memory limits rather than data scarcity, scaling disk bandwidth solves I/O stalls rather than token information quality, and eliminating synthetic generation ignores a key strategy for expanding scarce data pools.

    Learning Objective: Analyze the systems implications of the compute-to-data scaling asymmetry and diagnose the data wall regime.

  2. True or False: Doubling the amount of raw, deduplicated web data will always double the total information learned by the model.

    Answer: False. The section argues for diminishing returns, not a universal doubling law: additional data can add much less marginal signal than its extra compute cost. That is why teams must evaluate learning curves and target quality empirically instead of assuming linear gains from corpus size.

    Learning Objective: Evaluate the misconception that information value scales linearly with raw corpus volume.

  3. Order the following stages as they are presented in this chapter’s conceptual map of the data-selection pipeline: (1) Dynamic Selection, (2) Static Pruning, (3) Synthetic Data Generation.

    Answer: The correct order is: (2) Static Pruning, (1) Dynamic Selection, (3) Synthetic Data Generation. This is the chapter’s presentation order: prune low-value data before target training, adapt sample usage during training, then create missing coverage when needed. The sequence is a conceptual map rather than a mandatory runtime chronology, because synthetic data can also be created earlier and then pruned or selected before final training.

    Learning Objective: Identify the chapter’s conceptual ordering of the three main data-selection intervention points.

← Back to Questions

Self-Check: Answer

  1. Under the illustrative analytical model where dataset information content scales as \(\mathcal{I}(D) \propto \log D\) and training compute scales linearly as \(O = O_{\text{sample}} \cdot D\), how does the marginal Information-Compute Ratio \(\mathrm{ICR}(D)\) scale with dataset size \(D\)?

    1. It remains constant at \(\mathcal{O}(1)\) because additional compute scales proportionally with dataset size
    2. It decays as \(\mathcal{O}\!\left(\frac{1}{O_{\text{sample}}D}\right)\), so under this illustrative model additional data eventually adds compute faster than marginal information
    3. It grows logarithmically as \(\mathcal{O}(\log D / O_{\text{sample}})\) due to power-law parameter scaling
    4. It decays exponentially as \(\mathcal{O}(\exp(-D))\) once the training corpus exceeds accelerator memory capacity

    Answer: The correct answer is B. Under the chapter’s illustrative model, \(d\mathcal{I}/dD \propto 1/D\) and \(dO/dD = O_{\text{sample}}\), so their ratio is \(\mathrm{ICR}(D) \approx 1/(O_{\text{sample}}D)\). This captures diminishing marginal learning signal under the stated log-information and linear-compute assumptions. A constant or growing answer ignores the derivative of \(\log D\), while the exponential-decay choice introduces a memory-capacity mechanism that is not part of this model.

    Learning Objective: Calculate the scaling behavior of the Information-Compute Ratio under the section’s illustrative logarithmic-information model.

  2. Explain why a dataset where \(100\%\) of the sample labels are verifiably correct can still exhibit a very low Information-Compute Ratio (ICR).

    Answer: Label correctness measures ground-truth accuracy, whereas ICR measures evaluation gain per unit of training compute over a stated interval. A dataset of completely correct examples will have near-zero ICR if the samples are redundant, easily classified by the current model state, or already mastered, because processing them consumes forward and backward FLOPs without producing a corresponding evaluation gain; parameter updates alone do not establish learning value.

    Learning Objective: Compare data correctness with data value in the Information-Compute Ratio framework.

  3. An ML systems diagnostic plot maps normalized training compute on the horizontal axis against model performance on the vertical axis. A 10M-parameter run sits well below a fitted frontier under a fixed evaluation protocol and fixed total budget. A controlled sweep then shows that reallocating the same budget toward a larger model improves performance. What is the most defensible diagnosis of the original operating point?

    1. Data-starved regime
    2. I/O bandwidth-saturated regime
    3. The model/data allocation was suboptimal under the tested budget
    4. Over-echoing regime

    Answer: The correct answer is C. Under the chapter’s measurement framework, a point below a fitted frontier does not identify a unique cause by itself. What the controlled sweep supports is that the original allocation of budget across model size and data was suboptimal for the tested regime. Calling it purely data-starved or purely capacity-limited would overclaim from one observation, and the other choices describe different bottlenecks entirely.

    Learning Objective: Interpret a controlled compute-optimal-frontier sweep without over-inferring a unique bottleneck from a single operating point.

  4. A team wants one metric for how much smaller a selected dataset is at a target accuracy, and another for how much learning is gained per sample over a measured interval. Which pair of chapter metrics matches that need?

    1. Data Curation Rate and Accuracy Reduction Ratio
    2. Data Compression Ratio and Performance-Per-Data
    3. Dynamic Checkpoint Rate and Active Retention Rate
    4. Data Convergence Ratio and Amortized Risk Ratio

    Answer: The correct answer is B. Data Compression Ratio (DCR) asks how much the dataset can be reduced while still reaching a target accuracy, and Performance-Per-Data (PPD) measures accuracy gain per sample over a stated interval. The other choices introduce names the chapter does not use. AULC is another valid core metric, but it summarizes performance across dataset sizes rather than serving as the per-sample interval metric in the question.

    Learning Objective: Match the chapter’s core metrics to the measurement goals they are designed to answer.

  5. Why is Area Under the Learning Curve (AULC) a more informative metric than single-point final validation accuracy when comparing data selection methods across dataset sizes?

    Answer: AULC integrates performance over dataset size \(n\), so it rewards methods that achieve stronger accuracy earlier in the data-allocation range rather than judging them only at one endpoint. Under the same evaluation protocol and integration limits, a higher AULC means better aggregate performance over that same dataset-size range. The practical caveat is that this does not automatically imply the method is better at every dataset size, nor does it guarantee lower total FLOPs if per-sample cost differs across methods.

    Learning Objective: Justify AULC as a data-efficiency metric by relating it to performance across dataset sizes rather than a single endpoint.

  6. A training team reduces a dataset from \(1{,}000{,}000\) to \(100{,}000\) images. Full-data training takes 10 hours, subset training takes 1 hour, and scoring the full pool with the production model takes 12 hours. Which conclusion and next step best fit the selection inequality?

    1. Yes, the inequality is satisfied because a \(90\%\) dataset reduction guarantees positive ROI
    2. Yes, because the 9 hours saved in training outweigh the scoring cost; the team only needs more GPUs
    3. No, because \(12+1=13\) hours exceeds the 10-hour baseline; a plausible fix is proxy scoring or cached embeddings, but the team must verify that scoring drops below 9 hours while target quality holds
    4. No, because coreset training always increases memory-bandwidth consumption; the team should switch from NVMe SSDs to HDDs

    Answer: The correct answer is C. The chapter’s time-saving test is \(T_{\text{selection}}+T_{\text{train}}(D_{\text{subset}})<T_{\text{train}}(D_{\text{total}})\). Here, \(12+1=13\) hours, which is worse than the 10-hour baseline. Proxy models or cached embeddings are sensible candidate fixes, but they only restore training-time saving if measured scoring time falls below the 9-hour savings window and the selected subset still preserves target quality. A large reduction ratio alone does not prove success, and slower storage would move in the wrong direction.

    Learning Objective: Apply the selection inequality to diagnose when scoring overhead erases subset-training savings and identify what must be re-measured after a proposed fix.

← Back to Questions

Self-Check: Answer

  1. A team wants to score a dataset cheaply before training a large production model. They plan to train a small proxy for a few epochs and rank examples by prediction error, knowing that confidently wrong predictions can still score highly. Which method best matches that plan and its trade-off?

    1. \(k\)-Center clustering on raw pixel inputs, because it guarantees zero-cost label-aware boundary identification
    2. Forgetting Events scoring on the full production model, because it requires no proxy architecture and computes in \(\mathcal{O}(1)\) time
    3. EL2N (Error L2-Norm) scoring with a briefly trained proxy model, accepting that high error can reflect hard examples or noise
    4. Herding on Gaussian-distributed features, because it completely avoids computing feature representations

    Answer: The correct answer is C. EL2N ranks samples by early prediction error \(\lVert \hat{\mathbf{y}}-\mathbf{y} \rVert_2\), so a cheap proxy can score examples without a full target-model run. The section also warns that high EL2N can include noisy samples and that transfer from proxy to target is useful but not guaranteed. Geometry-based \(k\)-Center is not label-aware boundary scoring, Forgetting Events is much more expensive, and Herding still depends on feature representations.

    Learning Objective: Compare proxy-based EL2N scoring with other coreset methods in terms of cost, signal, and validation needs.

  2. Why is Locality-Sensitive Hashing (LSH) with MinHash preferred over exhaustive pairwise Jaccard similarity comparison for large-scale text deduplication?

    1. MinHash LSH guarantees \(100\%\) precision in detecting semantic paraphrases across different natural languages
    2. MinHash eliminates the need to tokenize or shingle input documents before hashing
    3. Exhaustive pairwise Jaccard comparison requires training a deep neural network, whereas LSH is purely rule-based
    4. Exhaustive pairwise comparison requires \(\mathcal{O}(D^2)\) document comparisons, whereas MinHash LSH hashes compact signatures into sublinear candidate collision buckets

    Answer: The correct answer is D. Comparing all pairs in a dataset of \(D\) documents requires \(\mathcal{O}(D^2)\) comparisons, which is computationally intractable for web-scale datasets (\(D \ge 10^7\)). MinHash compresses documents into fixed-size sketches where collision probability equals Jaccard similarity, and LSH bands these sketches into hash buckets so only candidate pairs colliding in a bucket are compared. MinHash LSH does not detect cross-lingual semantic paraphrases, still requires shingling/tokenization, and pairwise Jaccard comparison is an exact set operation rather than a deep learning method.

    Learning Objective: Analyze the computational scaling benefits of MinHash and Locality-Sensitive Hashing for web-scale deduplication.

  3. True or False: Removing near-duplicate documents with MinHash LSH always improves model accuracy because duplicate data has zero statistical value in all training regimes.

    Answer: False. While deduplication reduces redundant computation and memorization risk, in some domains duplicate frequency reflects natural empirical data distributions or intentional weighting. Aggressive deduplication with miscalibrated thresholds can prune legitimate variations or distort class priors, necessitating empirical validation on downstream accuracy.

    Learning Objective: Evaluate the systems and statistical trade-offs of aggressive near-duplicate filtering.

  4. The training-dynamics coreset metric that measures the Euclidean norm of the difference between predicted class probabilities and the one-hot target vector early in training is known as ____.

    Answer: EL2N. It measures \(\lVert p(x)-y \rVert_2\) early in training as an error-based difficulty score. The chapter notes that proxy-model rankings can transfer usefully to larger targets, but that transfer must be validated rather than assumed universal.

    Learning Objective: Identify EL2N from its mathematical definition and explain why proxy-based use still requires validation.

Self-Check: Answer

  1. In curriculum learning, an engineer implements an easy-to-hard pacing schedule that controls the fraction of the sorted training pool available at each stage. What is the primary objective of this pacing strategy?

    1. To guide early optimization with more stable examples before gradually introducing harder ones
    2. To eliminate the need for backward passes during the first half of training
    3. To maximize GPU memory bandwidth utilization by sorting tensors strictly by length in bytes
    4. To replace human labelers with an automated oracle during the late stages of training

    Answer: The correct answer is A. The chapter presents curriculum learning as a way to shape the optimization trajectory: easier examples can provide steadier early gradients, while harder examples are introduced later as the model matures. That is not a guarantee against divergence on every task, and ‘easy’ depends on the scoring rule, but it is the pacing mechanism this section teaches. The other choices confuse curriculum with memory-layout tricks or active-learning workflows.

    Learning Objective: Analyze how curriculum pacing can stabilize early learning by controlling when harder examples appear.

  2. An active learning pipeline chooses unlabeled examples for costly radiologist annotation. The team notices that simple uncertainty sampling repeatedly selects images from a single ambiguous artifact class, starving other disease categories. Which query strategy should they adopt to resolve this pathology?

    1. Least-confidence sampling, because it strictly selects the lowest top-1 probability prediction
    2. Diversity sampling or hybrid uncertainty-diversity sampling (such as BADGE), which balances uncertainty near decision boundaries with feature-space coverage
    3. Random undersampling of the entire unlabeled pool to reduce dataset size before scoring
    4. Uniform zero-shot pseudo-labeling without confidence thresholds

    Answer: The correct answer is B. Pure uncertainty sampling often suffers from sampling bias by selecting clustered points near a single ambiguous boundary region. Diversity sampling (or hybrid strategies like BADGE that incorporate gradient embeddings) ensures that selected queries span diverse clusters across the feature space while still targeting model uncertainty. Least-confidence sampling exacerbates the clustering issue, random undersampling discards informative candidates arbitrarily, and unthresholded pseudo-labeling introduces catastrophic label noise.

    Learning Objective: Compare active learning query strategies to mitigate sampling bias and redundancy.

  3. Order the steps in an active learning closed-loop iteration: (1) Select top query samples via query strategy, (2) Acquire expert annotations from the oracle, (3) Score unlabeled pool using current model, (4) Retrain or update model on expanded dataset, (5) Add newly labeled samples to the training set.

    Answer: The correct order is: (3) Score unlabeled pool using current model, (1) Select top query samples via query strategy, (2) Acquire expert annotations from the oracle, (5) Add newly labeled samples to the training set, (4) Retrain or update model on expanded dataset. The cycle begins with proxy or model scoring over the candidate pool, selecting candidate queries, querying the human oracle, incorporating verified labels into the dataset, and updating the model.

    Learning Objective: Design the closed-loop workflow of an iterative active learning system.

Self-Check: Answer

  1. What causes the phenomenon of ‘model collapse’ when generative models are trained recursively on synthetic data generated by earlier model iterations?

    1. GPU memory fragmentation caused by variable-length synthetic sequences during distributed training
    2. Overfitting to floating-point rounding errors during fp16 mixed-precision matrix multiplication
    3. A failure of the I/O storage subsystem to deliver synthetic batches at line rate
    4. Systematic underrepresentation and erosion of tail patterns across generations, so recursive retraining compounds missing or distorted rare cases

    Answer: The correct answer is D. The section’s model-collapse mechanism is recursive tail erosion: generated data underrepresents rare but important patterns, and retraining on later synthetic generations compounds those omissions and errors. High-probability modes dominating the sample stream is part of that story, but it does not prove universal collapse under every sampling regime. The other choices are execution problems, not the chapter’s statistical mechanism.

    Learning Objective: Analyze how recursive synthetic training can degrade tail coverage and diversity over generations.

  2. A team distills a ResNet-50 student on ImageNet and chooses offline distillation, precomputing the teacher’s FP32 logits for every training image instead of keeping the teacher in accelerator memory. Which cost does this choice move onto the data path?

    1. Every epoch must read per-class logit vectors in place of single integer labels, multiplying the label bytes the storage tier and input pipeline must deliver
    2. Every training step must run a teacher forward pass beside the student, restricting the student’s per-accelerator batch size
    3. The student must be trained with a lower learning rate, because soft targets produce larger gradients than one-hot labels
    4. The teacher must be retrained once per student epoch, so preparation cost grows linearly with the number of epochs

    Answer: The correct answer is A. Offline distillation stores one logit per class for every sample, so the per-sample label grows from a single integer index to four bytes per class, an expansion of more than three orders of magnitude for ImageNet’s thousand classes, and every epoch reads it back through the input pipeline. Teacher residency and the reduced student batch size are the costs of online distillation, which this choice avoids. Soft targets do not force a lower learning rate, and the teacher pass runs once during precomputation rather than once per epoch.

    Learning Objective: Analyze knowledge distillation as a data-synthesis lever by locating its storage and input-pipeline cost.

  3. Why is training exclusively on \(100\%\) synthetic data from a simulation engine often suboptimal for real-world deployment, and how does synthetic-to-real data mixing bridge this gap?

    Answer: Simulation engines have an inherent domain gap (\(\mathcal{D}_{\text{synth}} \ne \mathcal{D}_{\text{real}}\)) caused by simplified physics, missing sensor artifacts, and unmodeled environmental noise. Mixing synthetic data with a curated set of real-world examples anchors the model to real feature distributions while using synthetic samples to expand volume, cover edge cases, and balance rare classes.

    Learning Objective: Analyze domain gap challenges and justify synthetic-to-real mixing ratios.

  4. True or False: Using a high-capacity diffusion model or state-of-the-art LLM to generate synthetic training data guarantees that the resulting training set will be completely free of distribution shift relative to the real deployment environment.

    Answer: False. Generator quality does not eliminate domain gap. Synthetic generators can amplify subtle distribution shifts, introduce generator-specific artifacts, and omit edge cases present in real-world deployment environments, requiring domain validation against real ground truth.

    Learning Objective: Evaluate the misconception that advanced generative models eliminate domain shift.

← Back to Questions

Self-Check: Answer

  1. Explain the mechanism of confirmation bias in semi-supervised pseudo-labeling, and specify how confidence thresholding mitigates it.

    Answer: Confirmation bias arises when the model trains on its own wrong pseudo-labels and reinforces those mistakes in later rounds. A high confidence threshold filters out low-confidence predictions, but confidently wrong pseudo-labels can still survive, so thresholding is only a partial defense. The practical check is to compare the model’s confidence against held-out labeled validation evidence before trusting the expanded training set.

    Learning Objective: Explain confirmation bias in pseudo-labeling and evaluate what confidence thresholding can and cannot fix.

  2. True or False: Consistency regularization methods such as FixMatch assume that label-preserving perturbations of an input should not change the model’s predicted class distribution much.

    Answer: True. The method relies on a task-valid smoothness assumption: if the augmentation preserves the label, predictions across the two views should stay consistent. The key caveat is that closeness in raw input space alone is not enough—rotations, crops, or other perturbations can change the true label on some tasks.

    Learning Objective: Evaluate the task-dependent assumption behind consistency regularization.

  3. In the economics of foundation models, an organization invests \(C_{\text{pretrain}} = 10{,}000\) GPU-hours in self-supervised pretraining. Each downstream task fine-tuning costs \(C_{\text{finetune}} = 50\) GPU-hours. Training each task from scratch would cost \(C_{\text{scratch}} = 1{,}000\) GPU-hours. What is the minimum number of downstream tasks \(N^*\) required to break even on the pretraining investment?

    1. \(N^* = 5\) downstream tasks
    2. \(N^* = 8\) downstream tasks
    3. \(N^* = 11\) downstream tasks (\(10{,}000 + 11 \times 50 = 10{,}550 < 11 \times 1{,}000 = 11{,}000\))
    4. \(N^* = 50\) downstream tasks

    Answer: The correct answer is C. The break-even condition is \(C_{\text{pretrain}} + N \cdot C_{\text{finetune}} \le N \cdot C_{\text{scratch}}\), which gives \(10{,}000 + 50N \le 1000N\), or \(950N \ge 10{,}000\). Solving for \(N\) yields \(N \ge 10{,}000 / 950 \approx 10.53\). Thus, at least 11 downstream tasks are required for the foundation model approach to use fewer total GPU-hours under these assumptions. This is a compute-only crossover at matched target quality; a dollar comparison must also price labels, accelerator hours, and other differing costs. At 10 tasks, scratch training costs \(10{,}000\) hours while the foundation model costs \(10{,}500\) hours. At 11 tasks, scratch training costs \(11{,}000\) hours while the foundation model costs \(10{,}550\) hours.

    Learning Objective: Calculate the break-even threshold for amortizing self-supervised pretraining across downstream tasks.

  4. How does the MoCo (Momentum Contrast) framework reduce the hardware and memory constraints of contrastive self-supervised learning compared to naive SimCLR?

    1. By replacing convolutional backbones with rule-based lookup tables to avoid backpropagation
    2. By requiring fully supervised class labels to filter out false negative pairs
    3. By using a dynamic queue of negative keys and a slowly updating momentum encoder, decoupling negative dictionary size from mini-batch size
    4. By computing exact pairwise Jaccard similarities on raw byte sequences rather than latent embeddings

    Answer: The correct answer is C. Contrastive learning requires large sets of negative examples for effective representation learning. While SimCLR scales the mini-batch size (batches of up to 8,192 examples, paid for in activation memory and all-gather traffic), MoCo decouples the dictionary size from the mini-batch size by maintaining a memory queue of negative keys updated with a momentum-averaged teacher encoder. MoCo still uses neural backbones and backpropagation, operates in an unsupervised manner without human labels, and computes cosine similarities in latent space rather than raw byte Jaccard comparisons.

    Learning Objective: Compare contrastive self-supervised learning architectures and their memory-compute scaling trade-offs.

  5. What is the systems-level ‘homogenization risk’ (or blast radius) of pretraining a shared foundation model on an uncurated dataset?

    Answer: A shared base model can propagate the same upstream defect into many downstream tasks, so a bad pretraining-data choice has a much larger blast radius than in train-from-scratch workflows. For example, a memorized private pattern or systematic bias in the base model may affect many adaptations built on top of it. The actual impact is not universal, because downstream fine-tuning and validation can reduce or expose some defects.

    Learning Objective: Analyze why pretraining-data defects in a shared foundation model can affect many downstream adaptations.

  6. True or False: Because self-supervised pretraining learns general representations from unlabeled data, it completely eliminates the need for data selection or quality filtering during the pretraining stage.

    Answer: False. Self-supervised pretraining at web scale remains highly sensitive to pretraining data quality; uncurated corpora containing boilerplate, corrupted text, toxic samples, or pervasive duplicates degrade representation quality and inflate training FLOPs without improving downstream transfer.

    Learning Objective: Evaluate the misconception that self-supervised learning eliminates data curation requirements.

← Back to Questions

Self-Check: Answer

  1. Why do naive random sample lookups across non-contiguous indices in large un-sharded dataset files severely degrade I/O throughput on storage hardware, and how do shuffle buffers mitigate this?

    1. Random lookups bypass host CPU caches, forcing floating-point registers to re-encode all labels
    2. Random lookups violate PCIe parity checks, causing GPU kernel timeouts during backward passes
    3. Random lookups trigger hash collisions in the Python garbage collector, halting dataloading threads
    4. Random 4 KB reads fall below peak sequential storage bandwidth due to IOPS limits, by hundreds of times on HDDs, whereas shuffle buffers read large sequential chunks and randomize locally in memory

    Answer: The correct answer is D. Storage devices (HDDs, SATA SSDs, NVMe SSDs, and cloud object stores) provide peak throughput under large, contiguous sequential reads. Non-contiguous random 4 KB reads cut realized bandwidth (about 375\(\times\) on HDDs, about 14\(\times\) on SATA SSDs, and under 2\(\times\) on NVMe SSDs). Shuffle buffers co-design data loading by reading large sequential shards into host RAM and performing pseudo-random shuffling locally within memory, preserving peak sequential disk bandwidth. The other choices describe fictitious hardware or software failures.

    Learning Objective: Analyze the hardware empathy principles governing storage I/O and shuffle buffer design.

  2. Contrast upstream data echoing (echoing before data augmentation) with downstream data echoing (echoing after augmentation) in terms of computational overhead and sample diversity.

    Answer: Upstream echoing amortizes work before augmentation and lets repeated passes receive fresh random augmentations, so it can preserve more view diversity while still reducing some repeated upstream overhead. Downstream echoing amortizes earlier work through augmentation, but repeats the same already-augmented tensors rather than creating new views. The practical implication is to place the echo point after the bottleneck stage whose work you want to amortize, then check whether the repeated data still preserves target quality.

    Learning Objective: Compare upstream and downstream echoing by how much pipeline work they amortize and how much sample diversity they preserve.

  3. True or False: If the GPU training step takes 20 ms and the CPU data loading/augmentation pipeline takes 10 ms (\(R = T_{\text{pipeline}} / T_{\text{GPU}} = 0.5\)), applying a data echoing factor of \(e = 2\) will double the end-to-end training throughput.

    Answer: False. When \(R < 1\), the training job is accelerator-bound (the data pipeline is already faster than the GPU), so the GPU is never starved for data. Applying data echoing in this regime provides zero throughput gain and merely feeds duplicate or stale data to the accelerator.

    Learning Objective: Evaluate pipeline balance conditions under which data echoing provides zero throughput improvement.

  4. On one host with two to eight accelerator workers, how can centralized coreset selection become a bottleneck, and what trade-off does hierarchical selection introduce?

    1. Centralized selection requires all workers to share a single GPU; hierarchical selection distributes weights across SSDs
    2. Centralized selection fails because sharding prevents network cards from transmitting floating-point values
    3. Centralized selection can bottleneck host memory and coordinator dispatch; hierarchical selection prunes locally per shard, risking loss of globally rare samples across shards
    4. Centralized selection eliminates gradient synchronization; hierarchical selection disables local backpropagation

    Answer: The correct answer is C. Centralized selection gathers candidate scores or embeddings at a coordinator process on the same host. Its memory demand and CPU dispatch can compete with decoding and batch preparation, so adding accelerator workers does not necessarily accelerate selection. Hierarchical selection distributes selection by having workers select local coresets on their shards before merging at the coordinator; however, this shard-local pruning can introduce distribution skew if rare classes or boundary cases are unevenly distributed across shards. The other choices state incorrect hardware, communication, or backpropagation limitations.

    Learning Objective: Compare centralized, shard-local, and hierarchical architectures for distributed coreset selection.

  5. In distributed active learning, Worker A scores candidate samples using checkpoint step \(t\), while Worker B has already advanced the shared model to step \(t+100\). What consistency challenge arises, and what is the best response?

    1. Worker A encounters deadlock in CUDA streams; disable autograd during scoring
    2. Worker B overwrites Worker A’s local storage; mount the dataset read-only
    3. Worker A’s GPU runs out of memory; reduce batch size to 1
    4. Worker A may produce stale rankings that differ from fresher ones; version checkpoints and choose the score-refresh interval from measured ranking agreement

    Answer: The correct answer is D. The issue is ranking staleness: uncertainty scores from an older checkpoint can differ from those produced by a newer model. The chapter’s remedy is not to assume such rankings are automatically invalid, but to make them reproducible with checkpoint versioning and to set refresh frequency from measured agreement decay. The other choices confuse the problem with autograd deadlock, storage overwrites, or memory exhaustion.

    Learning Objective: Analyze score staleness in distributed active learning and choose a reproducible refresh strategy based on measured rank agreement.

  6. Explain why performing independent, shard-local coreset pruning on an unstratified, partitioned dataset can cause minority class collapse during distributed training.

    Answer: When a dataset is sharded across workers without stratification, rare minority classes may appear in very few shards. If each worker independently selects its top-\(k\) coreset based on local metrics, workers with only a few minority samples may discard them as outliers, systematically erasing the minority class from the global training corpus.

    Learning Objective: Explain how unstratified data sharding leads to minority class loss during distributed pruning.
